# ZENOO Dog Wellness Course — All 30 Lessons

Nov 3, 2026 · @MK

A 30-lesson course for dog owners who feed fresh, home-cooked or raw food (or a mix), taught by Agota, founder of ZENOO, about 8 minutes per lesson. All 30 lessons are written in full, in English first and then in Lithuanian, ready for Agota's approval and vet review.

## Setup

**Audience:** owners who feed, or want to feed, real food: fresh cooked, home-cooked, raw, or a mix with kibble. The course never assumes raw feeding; where advice differs by feeding style, the lesson says so. ZENOO's freeze-dried range appears as one option, not the answer.

- **Voice:** Agota, founder of ZENOO, pet nutritionist and pet wellness advocate. Warm, practical, never preachy. Agota approves every lesson before it goes out in her name.

- **Personal stories:** the hooks open with everyday scenes rather than invented stories about Agota. Where her own experience would fit better, a note says *\[Agota: add your story here\]*.

- **Sources:** established veterinary nutrition references (FEDIAF, NRC, WSAVA) and peer-reviewed studies, listed at the end. ZENOO's own website claims are attributed to ZENOO, never presented as research.

- **Vet review:** every lesson is checked by a vet before launch. The course map flags which lessons need the closest review.

- **Claims rule (EU feed law):** lessons teach nutrition and when to see a vet. They never say a food or ingredient prevents, treats or cures a disease.

- **Languages:** English first, with a matching Lithuanian version.

- **Platform:** the 9-screen structure is brand-neutral, so the same template can carry AYW, ZENOO and future client courses.

## Lesson format

Nine screens, about 8 minutes. It follows the AYW template, with two changes for dog owners: Speak becomes **Do it with your dog**, and screen 8 always starts with **when to call the vet**.

| \#  | Screen                       | Mode   | What happens                                                      | Time    | CMS field                                              |
|-----|------------------------------|--------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|---------|--------------------------------------------------------|
| 1   | The hook                     | Read   | A scene every dog owner knows + "In this lesson"                  | 1 min   | introduction                                           |
| 2   | The science, simply          | Read   | What's going on inside the dog, in plain words                    | 1.5 min | teaching                                               |
| 3   | Listen                       | Listen | 60–90 sec audio from Agota + 1 check question                     | 1.5 min | listen                                                 |
| 4   | Quick check                  | Read   | 2 multiple-choice questions with feedback                         | 1 min   | quiz                                                   |
| 5   | Write                        | Write  | A short log or plan about the user's own dog, with a model answer | 1.5 min | practiceIntro + interactivePractice + practiceFeedback |
| 6   | Do it with your dog          | Do     | A hands-on check or task with the dog (photo optional)            | 1 min   | doWithDog (new)                                        |
| 7   | Go deeper                    | Read   | One advanced idea or ZENOO tip                                    | 1 min   | advancedConcept                                        |
| 8   | Vet check + today's practice | —      | Red-flag signs, a 24-hour action, 3 takeaways                     | 0.5 min | vetCheck (new) + lessonWrapUp                          |
| 9   | Achievement                  | —      | Badge, lesson x of 30, streak                                     | —       | achievement                                            |

The user's dog profile (name, age, weight, breed, diet) should fill in the lessons, so Agota can say "Check Luna's bowl" rather than "your dog's bowl". The profile and the Write answers also feed the ZENOO AI coach.

## Lesson 1 — The Poop Review

About 8 minutes. Vet review: High.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** The most honest health report you'll get today

It's 7 AM. You're half awake, holding a bag, waiting while your dog finds the perfect spot.

Most of us look away. Today I want you to look.

Hi, I'm Agota, founder of ZENOO. *\[Agota: add one line of your own story here, e.g. the moment poop first told you something about a dog's food.\]* Every morning your dog hands you a free health report. Its colour, shape and size tell you how well the food is digested, how the gut is doing, and sometimes that something needs a vet.

By the end of this lesson you'll be able to read it in about ten seconds.

**In this lesson:**

- The 1–7 poop score vets use

- What each colour means, and which ones need a vet

- Why dogs on fresh, home-cooked or raw food often poop less, and firmer

- How to start a simple 7-day poop log

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

**The score.** Many vets use a 1–7 faecal score. **1** is hard, dry pellets. **2** is the ideal: firm, segmented, holds its shape and leaves almost nothing behind when you pick it up. **3** is softer but still holds its form — fine for many dogs. **4–5** are soggy and lose shape. **6–7** are mush and water.

**The colour.** Healthy poop is chocolate brown, coloured by bile. Food can shift it a little: liver and other organ meats make it darker, pumpkin or carrot can tint it orange.

**The amount.** Poop is mostly what the body *couldn't* digest. Highly digestible food leaves less behind. A study comparing extruded kibble with mildly cooked and raw diets found the fresh and raw diets were better digested and produced less faecal output (Algya et al., 2018). That's why dogs on fresh, well-made home-cooked or raw food often poop less, and firmer.

**Why firm matters.** A well-formed stool presses gently on the two small anal glands as it passes, helping them empty naturally. Many vets and fresh-food feeders link firm stools with fewer anal-gland problems. Diet is one factor among several, but it's one you control every day.

### Screen 3 · Listen — The ten-second check

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> Next time you pick up after your dog, run this ten-second check.
>
> One: shape. Does it hold together like a log, maybe in segments? Or does it fall apart, or puddle?
>
> Two: the pick-up test. Does it lift cleanly, or leave a smear on the grass? A clean lift is a great sign.
>
> Three: colour. Chocolate brown is what we want. Darker after liver is normal. Black and sticky, or bright red blood, is not — that's a call to your vet.
>
> Four: extras. A little shine of mucus now and then can be normal. White rice-like bits, or anything that looks like spaghetti, can be worms.
>
> Five: compare. Is this normal for my dog? One odd poop after a walk through the bins is usually nothing. A pattern over two or three days is information.
>
> That's it. Shape, pick-up, colour, extras, compare. Ten seconds, every morning.

**Check question:** What does a clean "pick-up test" tell you?

- The dog needs more fibre

- **The stool is well formed, usually a good sign (correct)**

- The dog is dehydrated

- Nothing useful

**Feedback:** Right. A stool that lifts cleanly is usually well formed and well digested.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** On the 1–7 poop score, which number is usually the ideal?

- 1

- **2 (correct)**

- 5

- 7

*Correct:* Yes — firm, segmented and easy to pick up. *Incorrect:* Not quite. A 1 is too hard; 5–7 are too loose. The sweet spot is 2.

**Q2.** Your dog's poop is black and sticky, like tar. What should you do?

- Wait a week and see

- Add pumpkin to the food

- **Call your vet the same day (correct)**

- Switch to a new food

*Correct:* Exactly. Black, tarry stool can mean bleeding higher up in the gut. *Incorrect:* This one can't wait. Black, tarry stool can mean bleeding, so call your vet today.

### Screen 5 · Write — Start your poop log

**Practice intro:** One poop tells you little. Seven tell you a lot. Today you'll write the first entry in your dog's log.

**Instructions:** Write today's entry: score (1–7), colour, anything unusual, and what your dog ate in the last 24 hours.

**Weak example:** "Looked okay."

**Why it's weak:** "Okay" can't be compared tomorrow. A number and a colour can.

**Placeholder:** Score:… / Colour:… / Anything unusual:… / Food in last 24 h:…

**Model answer:** "Score: 3. Colour: medium brown. Unusual: a little mucus at the end. Food: usual dinner plus a piece of cheese from my son."

**Why it works:** It's specific, and it links the poop to what went in — so you can spot a pattern by day 7.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** On your next walk, score the poop using the 1–7 chart in the app.

1.  Look at the shape before you pick it up.

2.  Do the pick-up test.

3.  Check the colour in daylight if you can.

4.  Optional: take a photo for your log. Photos make changes much easier to see over a week.

**App note:** show the 1–7 chart with a simple illustration of each score, plus a colour strip.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Colour guide

| Colour                              | What it can mean                      | What to do                                          |
|-------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------|
| Chocolate brown                     | Normal                                | Nothing                                             |
| Darker brown                        | Organ meats like liver                | Normal if firm                                      |
| Black, tarry, sticky                | Possible bleeding high in the gut     | Vet the same day                                    |
| Red streaks or fresh blood          | Irritation or bleeding low in the gut | Once with straining: watch. Repeated, or a lot: vet |
| Red, jelly-like ("raspberry jam")   | Can be serious bowel inflammation     | Vet urgently                                        |
| Yellow or orange                    | Food passing fast, or pumpkin/carrot  | If it lasts more than 2 days: vet                   |
| Green                               | Usually grass                         | If it lasts, or poison is possible: vet             |
| Grey, greasy, bulky                 | Fat not being digested                | Vet                                                 |
| White, chalky, crumbly              | Too much bone (common in raw feeding) | Reduce bone                                         |
| White rice-like bits or "spaghetti" | Possible worms                        | Vet for deworming                                   |

**A note on raw feeding:** firm is good, but chalky white and crumbly means too much bone. The goal is still a 2, not a 1. Home-cooked diets: lots of soft, bulky poop often means too much rice, pasta or vegetables, and a sudden jump in fat can loosen stools too.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Call your vet if you see:**

- Black, tarry stool, or a lot of blood

- Diarrhoea for more than 24 hours (sooner for puppies, seniors and small dogs)

- Diarrhoea together with vomiting, tiredness or refusing food

- Straining with little or nothing coming out

- Worms, or a chance your dog ate something toxic

**For the next 7 days:** score every morning poop and log it. On day 7, look at the pattern.

**Key takeaways:**

- Score 2 is the target: firm, segmented, easy to pick up.

- Colour tells you a lot; black and tarry or lots of blood means call the vet.

- More digestible food usually means less poop, and firmer poop.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Poop Detective

**Progress:** Lesson 1 of 30 complete · Day 1 streak · Day 1 of your 7-day poop log

**Celebration text:** You now read your dog's daily health report better than most owners ever will. Next: what your dog's body actually needs from food.

## Lesson 2 — What Your Dog Really Needs: Protein, Fat and Carbs

About 9 minutes. Vet review: Standard.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** The number on the bag isn't telling you the whole story

You're in the pet shop, comparing two bags. One says 26% protein. The tin next to it says 8%.

Easy choice, right? The bag has more than three times the protein.

Actually, the tin may have *more*. The difference is water, and once you know the trick, you'll never read a label the same way again.

Today we go back to basics: what your dog's body actually needs from food, and how much of it.

**In this lesson:**

- The three energy nutrients: protein, fat and carbohydrate

- Why dogs have no set need for carbs

- The dry-matter trick for reading any label

- How many calories your own dog needs per day

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

**Protein** builds and repairs muscle, skin, coat, enzymes and the immune system. It's made of amino acids, and dogs must get **10 essential amino acids** from food because their bodies can't make them. Animal proteins supply all ten in good balance. European guidelines (FEDIAF) set a minimum of about 18 g of protein per 100 g of dry food for adult dogs. That's a floor, not a target, and many dogs do well on much more.

**Fat** is the most concentrated energy source, with about twice the calories of protein or carbs per gram. It carries vitamins A, D, E and K and supplies essential fatty acids: linoleic acid (omega-6) for skin, and EPA and DHA (omega-3) for the brain, joints and coat.

**Carbohydrates** give quick energy and fibre feeds the gut bacteria. But here's the surprise: the US National Research Council found **no minimum requirement for carbohydrate** in adult dogs, as long as the diet has enough protein and fat. A dog's liver can make the glucose it needs. Pregnant and nursing dogs are the exception, where some carbohydrate helps. Fibre is still useful for the gut, in sensible amounts.

**Put together:** a species-appropriate diet is built on quality animal protein and fat, with carbs and fibre as supporting players, not the base.

### Screen 3 · Listen — The water trick

**Audio script (Agota, ~85 sec):**

> Let's solve the pet-shop puzzle.
>
> Every label lists protein as a percentage of the food *as it is in the pack* — water included. Dry kibble is about 10% water. A tin can be nearly 80% water. Fresh food sits somewhere high. Freeze-dried food is very dry, until you add water at home.
>
> To compare fairly, take the water out. That's called the dry-matter basis.
>
> The rule is simple: divide the protein by what's left after the water.
>
> The bag: 26% protein, 10% moisture. Twenty-six divided by ninety. About 29% protein.
>
> The tin: 8% protein, 78% moisture. Eight divided by twenty-two. About 36% protein.
>
> So the tin that looked weaker actually has more protein per bite of real food.
>
> Next time you're comparing foods, find the moisture line first. It changes everything.

**Check question:** Why can't you compare protein percentages straight from two different labels?

- Brands measure protein differently

- **The water content is different, so you must take the water out first (correct)**

- Protein percentages are always rounded

- Only kibble lists protein

**Feedback:** Exactly. Convert both to dry matter, then compare.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** How many essential amino acids must dogs get from their food?

- 3

- **10 (correct)**

- 22

- None

*Correct:* Yes — ten that the body can't make on its own. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Dogs need 10 essential amino acids from food.

**Q2.** According to the US National Research Council, what's the minimum carbohydrate requirement for a healthy adult dog?

- 30% of the diet

- 50% of the diet

- **No minimum has been set, as long as protein and fat are sufficient (correct)**

- Carbs must be the main ingredient

*Correct:* Right. The liver can make the glucose an adult dog needs. *Incorrect:* Surprising, but true: no minimum has been set for healthy adult dogs.

### Screen 5 · Write — Decode your dog's food

**Practice intro:** Grab the pack of the food your dog eats now. Find the lines called "analytical constituents". Cooking at home? Use a bought food you've considered, or go straight to the calorie task on the next screen. Lesson 6 covers balancing home recipes.

**Instructions:** Write down protein %, fat % and moisture %. Then work out protein on a dry-matter basis: protein ÷ (100 − moisture) × 100.

**Weak example:** "Protein 24%. Seems fine."

**Why it's weak:** Without moisture, 24% could mean very different things.

**Placeholder:** Protein:…% / Fat:…% / Moisture:…% / Protein on dry matter:…%

**Model answer:** "Protein 24%, fat 14%, moisture 8%. Dry matter protein: 24 ÷ 92 × 100 = 26%. Above the minimum, but less than I'd like for my active dog."

**Why it works:** It turns the label into a fair number and judges it against your own dog.

**App note:** add a built-in calculator so the user only types the three numbers.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** Weigh your dog and find their daily calorie needs.

1.  Weigh yourself, then weigh yourself holding your dog. The difference is your dog's weight. (Small dogs: a kitchen or baby scale works.)

2.  Enter the weight in the app. It calculates the **resting energy requirement**: 70 × weight in kg^0.75.

3.  For a typical neutered adult, daily needs are about **1.6 × resting energy**. Active dogs need more, couch dogs and seniors less.

4.  Compare it with the calories in what you feed now (kcal per 100 g on the pack).

**Example:** a 20 kg dog has a resting need of about 660 kcal, and a typical daily need of about 1,060 kcal.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — The calculator is a starting point

Every dog is different. Breed, age, coat, neutering, temperature and activity can move real needs up or down by 20–30%.

The best judge is your dog's body. You should be able to feel the ribs easily under a thin layer of fat, and see a waist from above. If your dog is gaining or losing weight, adjust portions by about 10% and re-weigh in two weeks. Lesson 15 teaches the full body-condition score.

A note on portions: dry and freeze-dried foods pack a lot of calories into a small scoop, while fresh and home-cooked food is mostly water, so the same calories look like a much bigger bowl. Feed by weight and calories, never by eye.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Talk to your vet if:**

- Your dog gains or loses more than 10% of body weight without a clear reason

- Your dog is suddenly much hungrier or thirstier than usual

- You're planning big diet changes for a puppy, a pregnant dog or a dog with a health condition

**For the next 24 hours:** weigh your dog, save the weight in their profile, and compare today's portion with their calorie needs.

**Key takeaways:**

- Quality animal protein and fat are the foundation; dogs have no set need for carbs.

- Compare foods on a dry-matter basis — water hides the truth.

- Calories are a starting point; your dog's body shape is the final answer.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Label Decoder

**Progress:** Lesson 2 of 30 complete · Day 2 streak

**Celebration text:** You can now read a pet food label better than most people who sell them. Next: the gut, and how to rebuild it after antibiotics.

## Lesson 3 — Prebiotics, Probiotics and Synbiotics

About 9 minutes. Vet review: High.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** The antibiotics worked. So why is the tummy still upset?

Your dog had an infection. The vet prescribed antibiotics, and they did their job.

But now the poop is soft, there's more gas than usual, and your dog seems a little off around food.

That's not your imagination. Antibiotics can't tell good bacteria from bad. They clear out the infection, and they knock back the helpful gut bacteria too. In some dogs, the gut takes weeks to recover.

The good news: you can help it rebuild. Today you'll learn how.

**In this lesson:**

- What the gut microbiome does for your dog

- Probiotics, prebiotics, synbiotics and postbiotics: the difference

- How to support the gut during and after antibiotics

- Gentle foods that feed good bacteria

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

Your dog's gut is home to trillions of bacteria and other microbes, together called the **microbiome**. They help digest food, make some vitamins and short-chain fatty acids that feed the gut lining, and train the immune system, much of which sits along the gut.

When this community gets knocked off balance, vets call it **dysbiosis**. A 2020 study found that after a course of the antibiotic metronidazole, healthy dogs' gut bacteria were still disturbed four weeks later in some cases (Pilla et al., 2020). Sudden diet changes and stress can upset the balance too.

The four words you'll see on labels:

- **Probiotics:** live microbes that, in the right amounts, benefit the host.

- **Prebiotics:** food for the good bacteria, usually certain fibres like inulin from chicory root.

- **Synbiotics:** probiotics and prebiotics together, so the good bacteria arrive with their own food.

- **Postbiotics:** helpful substances made by bacteria, or inactivated bacteria, that can benefit the gut without being alive.

**Put together:** probiotics bring in helpers, prebiotics feed them, and a steady, digestible diet gives them a stable home.

### Screen 3 · Listen — A gut-friendly antibiotic plan

**Audio script (Agota, ~85 sec):**

> If your dog is on antibiotics, here's a simple plan to discuss with your vet.
>
> First, finish the full course. Never stop antibiotics early because the poop looks better or worse. That's your vet's call.
>
> Second, ask your vet about a probiotic made for dogs. Give it at least two hours apart from the antibiotic dose, so the antibiotic doesn't knock it out straight away.
>
> Third, keep going after the course ends. Many vets suggest two to four more weeks of probiotic while the gut rebuilds.
>
> Fourth, keep the food steady. This is not the week to try a brand-new diet. Digestible, familiar meals are best.
>
> And fifth, add a little prebiotic food once the tummy settles: a spoonful of plain cooked pumpkin, for example.
>
> Watch the poop score from Lesson 1. It's the best progress report you've got.

**Check question:** How far apart should a probiotic and an antibiotic dose be given?

- At exactly the same time

- **At least two hours apart (correct)**

- One week apart

- It doesn't matter

**Feedback:** Right. Spacing them gives the probiotic a better chance to survive.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** What is a prebiotic?

- A live bacterium

- **Food, usually fibre, that feeds the good gut bacteria (correct)**

- A type of antibiotic

- A vitamin

*Correct:* Yes. Prebiotics feed the microbes; probiotics are the microbes. *Incorrect:* Close — that's the difference to remember: prebiotics feed, probiotics are the bacteria.

**Q2.** Your dog's poop gets softer during antibiotics. What should you do?

- Stop the antibiotics straight away

- Switch to a completely new food

- **Keep giving the antibiotics and call your vet for advice (correct)**

- Double the food portion

*Correct:* Exactly. Mild softness is common; your vet decides about the medication. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Never stop antibiotics yourself — call your vet.

### Screen 5 · Write — Your 2-week gut-support plan

**Practice intro:** A plan written down is a plan you'll actually follow.

**Instructions:** Write a 2-week gut plan for your dog: the probiotic (or the question you'll ask your vet), when you'll give it, one prebiotic food to add, and how you'll track progress.

**Weak example:** "Give some yoghurt."

**Why it's weak:** Yoghurt alone contains few strains in small amounts, and there's no timing, no plan and no tracking.

**Placeholder:** Probiotic:… / Timing:… / Prebiotic food:… / How I'll track:…

**Model answer:** "Probiotic: a dog-specific product my vet recommends, with the strains and amount listed on the label. Timing: with breakfast, 2+ hours after the morning antibiotic, and for 3 weeks after the course ends. Prebiotic food: a teaspoon of plain cooked pumpkin from day 5. Tracking: poop score every morning in the app."

**Why it works:** It's specific, it follows the vet's lead, and it has a built-in way to see if it's working.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** Read the label of a probiotic you have at home, or one you're thinking of buying.

1.  Does it name the **strains** (e.g. *Enterococcus faecium* followed by a strain code), not just "probiotic blend"?

2.  Does it state the **amount** of live microbes (CFU) per dose?

3.  Is it made for **dogs**?

4.  Is there an **expiry date** and storage instruction?

Three or four "yes" answers is a good sign. Fewer: ask your vet for an alternative.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Feeding the good bacteria with food

Small amounts of these foods can gently feed the microbiome:

- **Plain cooked pumpkin:** soluble fibre, helpful for both soft and hard stools

- **Chicory root and dandelion greens:** natural sources of inulin

- **Mushrooms such as shiitake (cooked):** beta-glucans (more in Lesson 10)

- **Plain kefir, a teaspoon or two:** fermented, with live cultures; skip it if your dog reacts to dairy

Go slowly. Too much fibre too fast causes gas and loose stools — the opposite of what you want.

ZENOO notes that its Turkey recipe contains natural prebiotics and is designed to be easy to digest. *\[Vet/Agota: confirm wording against the product spec.\]*

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Call your vet if:**

- Diarrhoea is severe, watery or lasts more than 24–48 hours during antibiotics

- You see blood, black stool, or vomiting

- Your dog stops eating or seems lethargic

- Your dog is a puppy, a senior or very small (they dehydrate faster)

**For the next 24 hours:** if your dog is on antibiotics, ask your vet the probiotic question. If not, read one probiotic label and keep scoring poop.

**Key takeaways:**

- Antibiotics can disturb the gut for weeks; probiotics and prebiotics help it rebuild.

- Space probiotics at least 2 hours from antibiotics and continue after the course.

- Never stop antibiotics yourself; your vet decides.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Gut Guardian

**Progress:** Lesson 3 of 30 complete · Day 3 streak

**Celebration text:** You now know more about the canine gut than most pet shop shelves. Next: vomiting versus regurgitation — when to relax and when to call the vet.

## Lesson 4 — Vomiting vs. Regurgitation

About 8 minutes. Vet review: Critical.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** It came back up. Now what?

You hear it from the other room: that unmistakable sound. You find a small pile on the floor and your dog looking slightly embarrassed.

Your heart jumps. Is this serious?

Most of the time, one episode is nothing to panic about. But *how* it came up, and *what* came up, tells you a lot. Vets ask about it first, so it's worth learning today.

**In this lesson:**

- The difference between vomiting and regurgitation

- What yellow foam on an empty stomach usually means

- Simple care after a single episode

- The signs that mean go to the vet now

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

**Regurgitation** is passive. Food comes back up from the oesophagus (the food pipe) before it reaches the stomach. There's no warning and no heaving: the dog lowers its head and it simply comes out. The food is usually undigested, often in a tube shape and coated in slime, and it often happens soon after eating. Gulping food is a common cause. Frequent regurgitation needs a vet, because it can point to a problem with the oesophagus, and food can be breathed into the lungs.

**Vomiting** is active. It comes from the stomach or upper intestine, and the body works hard to push it out. Warning signs come first: drooling, lip licking, restlessness, swallowing. Then the belly heaves. What comes up is partly digested, often with liquid or yellow bile.

**Yellow foam in the morning** on an empty stomach is often bile irritating the stomach after a long gap between meals. Many vets suggest a small late meal or splitting food into more meals — but recurring vomiting always deserves a vet check.

**Put together:** heaving means vomiting; no effort means regurgitation. Both are worth noting; patterns are worth a vet visit.

### Screen 3 · Listen — The three questions

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> When your dog brings something up, take a breath and ask three questions.
>
> One: was there effort? Heaving belly, retching, drooling first? That's vomiting. Just a quiet head-drop and out it came? That's regurgitation.
>
> Two: what does it look like? Whole, undigested food in a sausage shape points to regurgitation. Liquid, partly digested food or yellow bile points to vomiting.
>
> Three: how is your dog now? Bright, wagging, asking for breakfast? A single episode is often a simple stomach reset. Quiet, hiding, retching again, or a swollen belly? Call your vet.
>
> And one more tip: if it happens again, film it on your phone. A ten-second video helps your vet more than any description.

**Check question:** Your dog drools, licks its lips, then its belly heaves before food comes up. Which is it?

- Regurgitation

- **Vomiting (correct)**

- Coughing

- Normal digestion

**Feedback:** Right. Warning signs and heaving mean vomiting.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** Which sign points most to regurgitation?

- Yellow bile

- Strong belly heaving

- **Undigested food coming up with no effort, soon after eating (correct)**

- Drooling beforehand

*Correct:* Yes. No effort and undigested food are the classic signs. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Heaving, drooling and bile point to vomiting; effortless, undigested food points to regurgitation.

**Q2.** A large, deep-chested dog keeps trying to vomit but nothing comes up, and the belly looks swollen. What do you do?

- Wait until morning

- Offer water and food

- **Go to an emergency vet immediately (correct)**

- Give a probiotic

*Correct:* Exactly. This can be bloat (stomach twisting), a life-threatening emergency. *Incorrect:* This can't wait. Unproductive retching with a swollen belly can be bloat — go to the vet now.

### Screen 5 · Write — The episode log

**Practice intro:** When something comes up, a short note now saves guessing at the vet later.

**Instructions:** Write a log entry for the last time your dog vomited or regurgitated (or keep this template ready).

**Weak example:** "Threw up yesterday."

**Why it's weak:** It doesn't help a vet tell vomiting from regurgitation, or spot a cause.

**Placeholder:** When:… / Effort or no effort:… / What came up:… / Time since last meal:… / How the dog was after:…

**Model answer:** "Tuesday 7 AM. Effort: yes, heaving after lip licking. Came up: yellow foam, no food. Last meal: 13 hours before. After: bright, ate breakfast normally."

**Why it works:** It answers every question a vet will ask, and hints at a cause: a long gap since the last meal.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** Watch your dog eat their next meal.

1.  Time it. Does the bowl disappear in under a minute?

2.  Watch for gulping, coughing or bringing food back up.

3.  If your dog eats very fast, try spreading food on a lick mat or using a slow feeder (more in Lesson 19).

4.  Save the eating time in the app.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — After a single episode

If your adult dog vomited once and is otherwise bright and well:

- Leave fresh water available, offered in small amounts if they gulp.

- Let the stomach rest for a few hours, then offer a small, bland, easily digested meal, such as plain cooked chicken or turkey with a little cooked rice or pumpkin.

- Go back to normal food gradually over a day or two.

Don't do this with puppies, seniors, small breeds or dogs with health conditions: they can dehydrate or drop blood sugar quickly, so call your vet first.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Go to the vet now if:**

- Your dog retches without bringing anything up, especially with a swollen belly

- Vomiting happens more than 2–3 times in a day, or continues beyond 24 hours

- You see blood, or vomit that looks like coffee grounds

- Your dog may have swallowed a toy, bone, sock or something toxic

- Your dog can't keep water down, or is lethargic, in pain or also has diarrhoea

- Your dog is a puppy, a senior or has an existing condition

- Regurgitation happens often

**For the next 24 hours:** time one meal, and save the episode-log template in the app.

**Key takeaways:**

- Heaving means vomiting; no effort means regurgitation.

- One episode in a bright dog is often a reset; patterns need a vet.

- Unproductive retching with a swollen belly is an emergency.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Calm in a Crisis

**Progress:** Lesson 4 of 30 complete · Day 4 streak

**Celebration text:** You now know what to look for when your dog's stomach complains — and when not to wait. Next: choosing a feeding style that fits your dog and your life.

## Lesson 5 — Choosing a Feeding Style

About 9 minutes. Vet review: High.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** Raw, cooked, homemade, or a bit of everything?

Ask five dog owners how they feed, and you'll get five passionate answers. One swears by raw. One cooks every Sunday. One mixes fresh toppers into kibble. Each is sure they've found the right way.

Here's the honest truth: there isn't one right way. There's a right way **for your dog, your home and your week**.

Today you'll see the main feeding styles side by side, with what each does well and where each can go wrong.

**In this lesson:**

- The main styles: fresh cooked, home-cooked, raw (BARF and prey model) and mixed feeding

- The one word that matters most on any label: "complete"

- Where each style tends to fall short

- Food safety at home

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

| Style                              | What it is                                             | Strengths                                                   | Watch out for                                             |
|------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|
| Commercial fresh cooked            | Lightly cooked, refrigerated or frozen meals           | Digestible, convenient, safe                                | Price; check it's labelled "complete"                     |
| Commercial raw or freeze-dried raw | Frozen or freeze-dried raw meals                       | Digestible, minimal processing, often loved by picky eaters | Raw-meat hygiene; check "complete"                        |
| Home-cooked                        | You cook from a recipe                                 | Full control of ingredients                                 | Most home recipes miss key nutrients (Lesson 6)           |
| DIY raw: BARF                      | Raw meat, bone, organs, plus some vegetables and fruit | Fresh, varied                                               | Easy to get calcium, vitamins and minerals wrong          |
| DIY raw: Prey model (PMR)          | Raw meat, bone and organs only, no plants              | Simple ratios                                               | Gaps in vitamin E, D, zinc, iodine and omega-3 are common |
| Mixed feeding                      | A complete food plus fresh toppers                     | Easy upgrade, budget-friendly                               | Keep toppers to about 10% of calories                     |

**The word that matters most:** in the EU, a pet food labelled **complete** must cover all of a dog's nutritional needs on its own. **Complementary** foods (toppers, treats, many mixers) don't. If you feed only one thing, it must say complete.

**DIY ratios, for reference:** BARF diets are often built around roughly 70% muscle meat, 10% raw edible bone, 5% liver, 5% other organs, and small amounts of vegetables, fruit and seeds. Prey model raw drops the plants: roughly 80% muscle meat, 10% bone, 5% liver and 5% other organs. Ratios alone don't guarantee balance; the recipe still needs checking.

### Screen 3 · Listen — Choose for your real life

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> The best feeding style is the one you can do well, every day, for years.
>
> So ask yourself honestly.
>
> Time: do I have an hour a week to cook and portion? Or do I need something ready in a minute?
>
> Space: do I have freezer room? Or do I need food that sits on a shelf?
>
> Home: are there small children, elderly people, or anyone with a weak immune system? Raw meat needs very careful hygiene around them.
>
> Budget: what can I keep paying, month after month?
>
> And my dog: age, health, allergies, how fussy they are.
>
> There's no shame in mixing. A complete base food with fresh toppers is a brilliant step up for many families. What matters is that the whole bowl is complete and balanced, and that you can keep it going.

**Check question:** What does Agota say makes the "best" feeding style?

- Whatever is most natural

- The most expensive option

- **The one you can do well, every day, for years (correct)**

- Raw food only

**Feedback:** Right. Consistency beats perfection.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** In the EU, what does "complete" on a pet food label mean?

- It contains meat

- **It covers all the dog's nutritional needs on its own (correct)**

- It's organic

- It's suitable for puppies

*Correct:* Yes. Feed only "complete" foods as the main diet. *Incorrect:* Not quite. "Complete" means the food alone meets all nutritional needs.

**Q2.** What's the main difference between BARF and prey model raw?

- BARF is cooked

- **Prey model leaves out plants; BARF includes some vegetables and fruit (correct)**

- Prey model uses only fish

- There is no difference

*Correct:* Exactly. Both use meat, bone and organs; only BARF adds plants. *Incorrect:* Close — the key difference is that prey model leaves out vegetables and fruit.

### Screen 5 · Write — Your feeding style match

**Practice intro:** Let's match a style to your real life, not an ideal one.

**Instructions:** Answer the five questions from the audio in one line each, then write the feeding style that fits best.

**Weak example:** "I'll do raw because it's natural."

**Why it's weak:** It skips time, budget, home and the dog itself.

**Placeholder:** Time:… / Space:… / Home:… / Budget:… / My dog:… / Best fit:…

**Model answer:** "Time: 30 minutes a week at most. Space: small freezer. Home: toddler in the house. Budget: medium. My dog: 4-year-old Labrador, sensitive stomach. Best fit: a complete commercial fresh or freeze-dried food as the base, with cooked fresh toppers, and strict hygiene."

**Why it works:** It's honest about limits and protects both the dog and the family.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** Check what's in your dog's bowl today.

1.  Find the label of the main food. Does it say **complete** or **complementary**?

2.  If you cook at home, is your recipe from a vet nutritionist or a recipe tool that adds a vitamin-mineral supplement?

3.  Roughly what share of daily food comes from toppers and treats? Aim for no more than about 10% of calories.

4.  Save your answers in your dog's profile.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Kitchen safety

Raw meat, whether you prepare it yourself or buy it frozen, can carry bacteria such as Salmonella and Listeria, which can make people ill too.

- Thaw frozen raw food in the fridge, never on the counter.

- Wash hands, bowls and surfaces with hot soapy water after every meal.

- Pick up uneaten food within about 30 minutes.

- Store dog food away from food for people.

- Never feed cooked bones: they splinter. Raw bones also carry risks (broken teeth, choking, blockages), so ask your vet if they suit your dog.

Freeze-drying and gentle cooking reduce handling time but don't replace good hygiene.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Talk to your vet before switching if your dog:**

- Is a puppy, pregnant, nursing, elderly or underweight

- Has kidney, liver, pancreas, heart or digestive disease

- Lives with someone pregnant, very young, elderly or immunocompromised (for raw feeding)

**For the next 24 hours:** check the "complete" label and save your feeding style in the app.

**Key takeaways:**

- No single style is best; the best is the one you can do well, for years.

- The main food must be labelled "complete".

- Home-made and DIY raw diets need a properly balanced recipe.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Bowl Strategist

**Progress:** Lesson 5 of 30 complete · Day 5 streak

**Celebration text:** You've matched a feeding style to your real life. Next: how to make home-cooked food truly balanced.

## Lesson 6 — Balancing Home-Cooked Diets

About 9 minutes. Vet review: High.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** Chicken, rice and love. Is it enough?

A pot of chicken, rice and carrots on the stove. Your dog sits by the cooker, tail sweeping the floor. It smells like care, and it is.

But love doesn't add calcium. Studies that checked home-cooked dog recipes from books and websites found that **most of them were short of at least one essential nutrient**. The dog looks fine for months, while small gaps slowly add up.

Today you'll learn the four gaps that show up again and again, and how to close them.

**In this lesson:**

- Why home-cooked recipes so often fall short

- The "big four" gaps: calcium, zinc, vitamin D and iodine

- Other common misses

- How to get a recipe that's truly balanced

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

Dogs need around 40 nutrients in the right amounts. Meat, grains and vegetables cover many of them, but not all.

| Nutrient  | Why it matters                | Why home recipes miss it                                                                                        |
|-----------|-------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Calcium   | Bones, teeth, muscles, nerves | Meat is very low in calcium and high in phosphorus. Without bone or a calcium source, the balance is badly off. |
| Zinc      | Skin, coat, immunity, healing | Needs are high; plant fibre and too much calcium reduce absorption.                                             |
| Vitamin D | Calcium use and bones         | Dogs can't make enough from sunlight; few foods contain it.                                                     |
| Iodine    | Thyroid and metabolism        | Only a few foods (fish, kelp, iodised salt) provide it.                                                         |

**Other frequent gaps:** vitamin E, choline, copper, the omega-3s EPA and DHA, and sometimes too much vitamin A when liver is overused.

**The fix:** a recipe built by a **board-certified veterinary nutritionist** or a reputable recipe tool, plus a **vitamin-mineral supplement made to match that recipe**. A general multivitamin rarely fits.

### Screen 3 · Listen — Calcium, the quiet one

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> Let's talk about calcium, because it's the gap I see most.
>
> Meat is rich in phosphorus but has almost no calcium. In nature, a dog gets calcium by eating bone. In a cooking pot, there usually isn't any.
>
> When calcium stays low for months, the body pulls it from the skeleton. In adult dogs that can mean weaker bones. In puppies it can be serious, because their bones are still growing.
>
> Too much calcium is also a problem, especially for large-breed puppies.
>
> So I don't guess. If you cook at home, use a recipe that tells you exactly how much calcium to add and in what form, such as a measured calcium supplement, ground eggshell measured by weight, or a complete supplement designed for that recipe.
>
> A spoonful of guesswork can do more harm than good.

**Check question:** Why do home-cooked meat diets often lack calcium?

- Dogs don't need calcium

- Cooking destroys calcium

- **Meat contains very little calcium unless bone or a supplement is added (correct)**

- Rice blocks calcium

**Feedback:** Exactly. Meat alone is high in phosphorus and low in calcium.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** Which of these is NOT one of the "big four" home-cooking gaps?

- Calcium

- Zinc

- **Protein (correct)**

- Iodine

*Correct:* Right. Home recipes usually have plenty of protein; minerals and vitamins are the problem. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Protein is usually fine; calcium, zinc, vitamin D and iodine are the common gaps.

**Q2.** What's the safest way to balance a home-cooked diet?

- Add a human multivitamin

- Rotate many ingredients and hope it evens out

- **Use a recipe from a veterinary nutritionist with a supplement matched to it (correct)**

- Feed more liver

*Correct:* Yes. Variety helps, but it doesn't guarantee balance. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Variety and human vitamins can't reliably close the gaps; a matched recipe can.

### Screen 5 · Write — Check your recipe

**Practice intro:** Let's look at one recipe you use, or one you'd like to try.

**Instructions:** Write the ingredients, then mark which of the big four each one supplies.

**Weak example:** "Chicken, rice, carrots. Looks healthy."

**Why it's weak:** It doesn't check any nutrient. This recipe is short of calcium, vitamin D, iodine and probably zinc.

**Placeholder:** Ingredients:… / Calcium source:… / Zinc source:… / Vitamin D source:… / Iodine source:… / Missing:…

**Model answer:** "Ingredients: turkey mince, sweet potato, courgette, sunflower oil. Calcium source: none. Zinc: some from turkey, probably not enough. Vitamin D: none. Iodine: none. Missing: calcium, vitamin D, iodine and likely zinc and omega-3. Next step: ask a vet nutritionist to balance it or use a matched supplement."

**Why it works:** It's specific, honest about the gaps, and ends with a safe next step.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** Run the gap check on one home recipe.

1.  Write down every ingredient and amount for one day's meals.

2.  Tick off a source for calcium, zinc, vitamin D and iodine.

3.  Note anything missing in your dog's profile.

4.  If anything is missing and home food is more than about 10% of the diet, plan a balanced recipe with your vet or a vet nutritionist.

If home-cooked food is only a topper on a complete food, your dog's base already covers these needs. Keep toppers small.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Why "variety" isn't enough

A common idea is that rotating many foods will balance out over the week. It helps with some nutrients, but if *none* of the ingredients contains enough vitamin D or iodine, rotating them changes nothing. Gaps have to be filled on purpose.

Also be careful in the other direction: liver is rich in vitamin A and copper, and too much over time can cause harm. Fish-liver oils add vitamin D and A; doubling up with other supplements can overshoot. More is not always better.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Talk to your vet if your dog:**

- Is a puppy, especially a large breed, or pregnant or nursing (needs are very precise)

- Has kidney, liver or heart disease (some nutrients need limiting)

- Has been on an unbalanced home diet for months and shows dull coat, flaky skin, lameness or low energy

**For the next 24 hours:** run the gap check on one recipe.

**Key takeaways:**

- Most home recipes miss at least one nutrient.

- Calcium, zinc, vitamin D and iodine are the usual gaps.

- A vet-nutritionist recipe with a matched supplement closes them.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Recipe Detective

**Progress:** Lesson 6 of 30 complete · Day 6 streak

**Celebration text:** You've learned to look past the pot and into the nutrients. Next: how to switch foods without upsetting the tummy.

## Lesson 7 — Switching Food Safely

About 8 minutes. Vet review: High.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** New food, same happy tummy

You've found a better food. You're excited. You pour a full bowl of it tonight, and by tomorrow morning there's a mess on the kitchen floor.

It isn't that the new food is bad. The gut simply wasn't ready. The microbes in your dog's gut are tuned to what they've been eating, and they need time to adjust.

Today you'll learn a calm, step-by-step switch that works for fresh, home-cooked, freeze-dried or raw food.

**In this lesson:**

- Why sudden changes upset the gut

- A 7–14 day switching plan

- How to read the poop during the switch

- When to slow down and when to stop

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

The gut microbiome adapts to the food it receives. Different proteins, fat levels and fibres feed different microbes. A sudden change can cause loose stools, gas or vomiting while the microbes and digestive enzymes catch up.

**Standard switching plan:**

| Days | Old food | New food |
|------|----------|----------|
| 1–2  | 75%      | 25%      |
| 3–4  | 50%      | 50%      |
| 5–6  | 25%      | 75%      |
| 7+   | 0%       | 100%     |

For sensitive dogs, seniors, or big jumps (for example from dry kibble to raw, or from a low-fat to a higher-fat food), stretch each step to 3–4 days, for a 10–14 day switch.

**Tip:** fresh and raw foods are often more digestible and contain more water, so portions in grams can look larger than kibble. Follow the new food's feeding guide, and weigh rather than scoop.

### Screen 3 · Listen — Let the poop set the pace

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> The calendar is a guide. The poop is the boss.
>
> Each morning, look at your dog's stool and give it a score, just like in Lesson 1.
>
> If it's firm and normal, move to the next step.
>
> If it's a little soft, stay where you are for two or three more days before moving on.
>
> If it's runny, go back one step and wait until things settle.
>
> Some dogs have a day or two of slightly different poop: a change in colour or smell is normal with a new food. That alone isn't a reason to worry.
>
> And keep everything else steady during the switch: no new treats, no new chews, no new supplements. If something goes wrong, you'll know why.

**Check question:** What should you do if stool turns slightly soft during the switch?

- Stop the new food forever

- Jump straight to 100% new food

- **Stay on the current step for a few more days (correct)**

- Add new treats to help

**Feedback:** Right. Slow down; don't give up.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** How long does a standard food switch take?

- One meal

- **About 7 days, or 10–14 for sensitive dogs (correct)**

- Three months

- Two days

*Correct:* Yes. Give the gut time to adapt. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Most dogs need about a week, and sensitive dogs up to two.

**Q2.** Why keep treats and supplements unchanged during a switch?

- They stop working during a switch

- **So you can tell what caused any tummy upset (correct)**

- Dogs don't need treats

- Treats speed up the switch

*Correct:* Exactly. One change at a time. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Changing several things at once makes it impossible to know what caused a problem.

### Screen 5 · Write — Your switching plan

**Practice intro:** Plan the switch before you start.

**Instructions:** Write the dates for each step, the grams of old and new food per day, and what you'll do if the stool goes soft.

**Weak example:** "Mix them for a few days."

**Why it's weak:** No amounts, no dates, no plan B.

**Placeholder:** Days 1–2:… / Days 3–4:… / Days 5–6:… / Day 7:… / If soft stool:…

**Model answer:** "Dog: 15 kg. Daily total from the new food's guide: 300 g fresh food (old food: 200 g kibble). Days 1–2: 150 g kibble + 75 g fresh. Days 3–4: 100 g kibble + 150 g fresh. Days 5–6: 50 g kibble + 225 g fresh. Day 7: 300 g fresh. If soft: stay on the step for 3 more days. If runny: go back one step."

**Why it works:** Clear amounts, dates and a backup plan.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** Start day 1, or plan your start date.

1.  Weigh today's portion: 75% old food, 25% new.

2.  Note the date and portion in the app.

3.  Tomorrow morning, score the stool and log it.

4.  Set a reminder for the next step in two days.

Already happy on the right food? Use this lesson as your plan for the next time you change protein or brand.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Mixing raw and kibble

Some people say raw and kibble must never be fed in the same meal because they "digest at different speeds". Good studies haven't shown this to be harmful for most healthy dogs, and many dogs switch smoothly with mixed meals. If your dog is sensitive, you can feed them in separate meals during the switch (old food in the morning, new food in the evening) and adjust the ratio over the days.

For a very sensitive gut, a vet may suggest a short period on a bland diet or a probiotic before switching.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Stop the switch and call your vet if:**

- Diarrhoea lasts more than 24–48 hours, or contains blood

- Your dog vomits more than once or refuses food for a day

- Your dog is a puppy, senior or small breed and has loose stools for more than a day

- Your dog is lethargic, in pain, or the belly looks swollen

**For the next 24 hours:** weigh day 1 portions and log tomorrow's stool score.

**Key takeaways:**

- Switch over 7–14 days, in steps.

- Let the poop set the pace.

- One change at a time.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Smooth Switcher

**Progress:** Lesson 7 of 30 complete · Day 7 streak

**Celebration text:** A full week of learning, and a calm switching plan in your pocket. Next: what itchy paws and ears may be telling you.

## Lesson 8 — Paw Licking, Ears & the Gut

About 9 minutes. Vet review: Critical.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** That sound at night: lick, lick, lick

It's late. The house is quiet, except for one sound coming from the dog bed. Lick, lick, lick. In the morning there are reddish-brown stains between the toes, and your dog keeps shaking their head.

Itchy paws and ears are among the most common reasons dogs visit the vet. Sometimes it's boredom or a splinter. Often it's allergy, and in some dogs, food plays a part.

Today you'll learn what the signs may mean, how to check, and why a vet is your partner here.

**In this lesson:**

- The usual causes of itchy paws and ears

- How environmental and food allergies differ

- How the gut and skin are connected

- What a proper elimination diet looks like

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

**Common causes of paw licking and ear trouble:**

- **Environmental allergy (atopic dermatitis):** pollen, dust mites, moulds. The most common allergy in dogs. Often seasonal at first.

- **Food allergy or intolerance:** a reaction to a protein, most often beef, dairy, chicken or wheat. Less common than environmental allergy, but all year round.

- **Fleas:** even a few bites can cause intense itch in allergic dogs.

- **Yeast or bacterial infections:** often follow allergy; they cause smell, redness and brown staining.

- **Pain, boredom or anxiety:** licking one paw only can be pain or an injury.

**The gut and skin link:** the gut lining and skin are both barriers, and both talk to the immune system. Research shows allergic dogs often have a different gut microbiome to healthy dogs. A healthy gut doesn't cure allergy, but it is part of the bigger picture.

**The only reliable test for food allergy** is an **elimination diet**: 8 weeks on a single new protein (or a hydrolysed diet) with nothing else, then a challenge with the old food. Blood and hair tests for food allergy are not reliable.

### Screen 3 · Listen — Why "just change the food" isn't enough

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> Many owners change the food when their dog itches. Sometimes it seems to help, often it doesn't, and then they change it again. And again.
>
> Here's why. Most itchy dogs have an environmental allergy, not a food allergy. Changing the food won't fix pollen.
>
> And when it is food, the trial has to be strict. One new protein the dog has never eaten, for eight weeks. No treats, no chews, no flavoured medicines, no bites from the table. A single piece of cheese can spoil the result.
>
> Fresh, home-cooked or raw single-protein diets can work well for this, but only with your vet's plan, because a home elimination diet must still be balanced if it runs for weeks.
>
> So start with your vet. Rule out fleas and infection first. Then decide together if a food trial makes sense.

**Check question:** How long does a proper elimination diet trial usually last?

- 3 days

- 1 week

- **About 8 weeks (correct)**

- 1 year

**Feedback:** Right. The skin needs weeks to show the change.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** Which is the most common cause of allergic itching in dogs?

- Food allergy

- **Environmental allergy (correct)**

- Too much exercise

- Cold weather

*Correct:* Yes. Environmental allergy is more common than food allergy. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Food allergy exists, but environmental allergy is more common.

**Q2.** Which test reliably diagnoses food allergy in dogs?

- A hair test

- A saliva test

- **An elimination diet followed by a food challenge (correct)**

- A blood test sold online

*Correct:* Exactly. Hair and saliva tests aren't reliable. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Only a strict elimination diet and challenge is reliable.

### Screen 5 · Write — Your itch diary

**Practice intro:** Patterns help your vet more than anything.

**Instructions:** Describe the itch: where, when, how often, and anything that seems to make it better or worse.

**Weak example:** "He's itchy a lot."

**Why it's weak:** It gives the vet nothing to work with.

**Placeholder:** Where:… / When (season, time of day):… / How often:… / Better or worse with:… / Other signs:…

**Model answer:** "Where: front paws and both ears. When: worse from May to September, mostly in the evening. How often: licks paws 5–10 minutes several times a day. Better or worse: worse after walks in long grass. Other signs: brown staining between toes, ears smell yeasty. No change in poop."

**Why it works:** The seasonal pattern and grass link point towards an environmental cause, a useful clue for the vet.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** A gentle paw and ear check.

1.  Spread the toes of each paw. Look for redness, swelling, brown staining, cuts, grass seeds or broken nails.

2.  Lift each ear flap. Look for redness, dark wax, discharge, and smell it.

3.  Note your findings and take photos.

4.  Repeat weekly so you can spot changes early.

If your dog pulls away in pain, don't force it. That's a reason for a vet visit.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — What can help alongside your vet's plan

Once your vet has a plan, these may support the skin barrier:

- **Omega-3 fatty acids (EPA and DHA)** from fish or algae oil: research supports their role in skin health and allergic itch. Dosing should come from your vet (see Lesson 9).

- **Wiping paws after walks** with a damp cloth to remove pollen.

- **Regular flea prevention**, all year.

- **A complete, balanced diet** with good-quality protein and fats.

None of these replaces diagnosis. Itch that keeps returning deserves a proper investigation.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**See your vet promptly if:**

- Skin is broken, bleeding, oozing or very red

- The ear smells, has discharge, or your dog tilts their head or loses balance

- There's swelling of the face, lips or eyes, or hives (call urgently if breathing is affected)

- Licking is focused on one paw (possible injury or foreign body)

- Hair loss, or itch that stops your dog sleeping

**For the next 24 hours:** do the paw and ear check and start the itch diary.

**Key takeaways:**

- Most itchy dogs have environmental, not food, allergy.

- Food allergy is diagnosed by an 8-week elimination diet, not a hair or blood test.

- Gut and skin are connected, but your vet leads the plan.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Itch Investigator

**Progress:** Lesson 8 of 30 complete · Day 8 streak

**Celebration text:** You now know what to look for and what to ask. Next: the fats that make a coat shine.

## Lesson 9 — Omega-3 and Omega-6 Balance

About 8 minutes. Vet review: High.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** The shine you can see from across the park

You've seen that dog at the park: a coat that catches the light, soft to touch, no flakes on the collar. And maybe you've looked at your own dog's coat and thought, why is it so dull?

Often, part of the answer is fat. Not more fat, but the **right balance of fats**.

Today you'll learn about omega-3 and omega-6, why both matter, and how to tip the balance in your dog's favour.

**In this lesson:**

- What omega-3 and omega-6 fats do

- Why the balance matters

- The best sources for dogs

- Safe use of fish and algae oils

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

**Omega-6 fats** (mainly linoleic acid) are essential. They keep the skin barrier strong. Sources: chicken and pork fat, sunflower, corn and soybean oils. Most diets have plenty.

**Omega-3 fats** come in two groups:

- **ALA** from plants (flaxseed, chia, hemp). Dogs convert very little ALA into the useful forms.

- **EPA and DHA** from fish, mussels and algae. These are the ones that support skin, joints, the brain, the heart and the eyes.

**Why balance matters:** both families use the same body pathways. Omega-6 tends to be the building block of inflammatory signals; EPA and DHA tend to calm them. A diet heavy in omega-6 and light in EPA and DHA tips the balance towards inflammation. Many diets based on chicken and vegetable oils fall into this pattern.

**Good EPA and DHA sources:** small oily fish (sardines, anchovies, mackerel), salmon, green-lipped mussel, fish oil and algae oil. Grass-fed meat contains a little more omega-3 than grain-fed.

### Screen 3 · Listen — Fish oil, the right way

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> Fish oil is one of the best-studied supplements for dogs. But a few rules keep it safe.
>
> First, read the label for EPA plus DHA, not just "fish oil". One capsule of 1000 milligrams of fish oil might only have 300 milligrams of EPA and DHA.
>
> Second, ask your vet for a dose. The right amount depends on your dog's weight and the reason you're giving it. Too much can cause loose stools, a fishy smell, and in high doses it may affect blood clotting.
>
> Third, freshness. Omega-3 oils go rancid easily. Keep them cool and dark, use them within the date, and if it smells strongly off, throw it away.
>
> And fourth, start low. Begin with a quarter of the dose and build up over a week or two.
>
> Never use cod liver oil as your main omega-3 source unless your vet says so. It is very high in vitamins A and D.

**Check question:** What should you look for on a fish oil label?

- The colour of the capsule

- **The amount of EPA plus DHA (correct)**

- The word "natural"

- The size of the bottle

**Feedback:** Exactly. EPA and DHA are what count.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** Which omega-3s do dogs use best?

- ALA from flaxseed

- **EPA and DHA from fish, mussels or algae (correct)**

- Linoleic acid

- Coconut oil

*Correct:* Yes. Dogs convert little ALA, so marine sources matter. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Dogs use EPA and DHA from marine sources best.

**Q2.** Why is cod liver oil not ideal as a daily omega-3 source?

- It has no omega-3

- **It's very high in vitamins A and D, which can build up (correct)**

- Dogs don't like the taste

- It's always rancid

*Correct:* Right. Use a fish body oil or algae oil instead. *Incorrect:* Not quite. The problem is the high vitamin A and D content.

### Screen 5 · Write — Your fat audit

**Practice intro:** Let's look at the fats in your dog's bowl.

**Instructions:** List the fat sources in your dog's food and treats. Mark each as mainly omega-6, mainly omega-3 (EPA and DHA), or other.

**Weak example:** "There's oil in it, so it's fine."

**Why it's weak:** It doesn't say which fats or in what balance.

**Placeholder:** Fat sources:… / Omega-6:… / Omega-3 (EPA and DHA):… / Next step:…

**Model answer:** "Fat sources: chicken fat, sunflower oil, flaxseed. Omega-6: chicken fat and sunflower oil. Omega-3: flaxseed (ALA only, poorly used). EPA and DHA: none. Next step: add sardines twice a week or ask my vet about a fish or algae oil dose."

**Why it works:** It identifies the real gap and a practical next step.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** The coat-shine check.

1.  In natural daylight, look at your dog's coat. Is it shiny or dull?

2.  Run your hand against the grain. Any flakes, greasy patches or dry skin?

3.  Look at the collar and bedding for dandruff.

4.  Take a photo and give the coat a score from 1 (dull, flaky) to 5 (glossy, soft). Repeat in 6–8 weeks if you change the fat balance; skin and coat take time.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Beyond the coat

EPA and DHA are studied far beyond skin:

- **Joints:** clinical trials in dogs with osteoarthritis show improved mobility with omega-3-enriched diets (Lesson 13).

- **Brain:** DHA is a building block of the brain and eyes, important for puppies and seniors (Lessons 20, 21 and 28).

- **Kidneys and heart:** some therapeutic veterinary diets are enriched with omega-3.

Omega-3s aren't magic, but they're one of the few supplements with solid research behind them.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Talk to your vet before adding omega-3 oils if your dog:**

- Takes blood thinners or anti-inflammatory medicines, or is due for surgery

- Has pancreatitis or a history of it (extra fat can be risky)

- Has diarrhoea, or a clotting disorder

**For the next 24 hours:** do the coat-shine check and the fat audit.

**Key takeaways:**

- Dogs need both omega-6 and omega-3, in balance.

- EPA and DHA from marine sources are the useful omega-3s.

- Dose by EPA plus DHA, with your vet.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Shine Seeker

**Progress:** Lesson 9 of 30 complete · Day 9 streak

**Celebration text:** You can now read a fat label like a pro. Next: the world of functional mushrooms.

## Lesson 10 — Functional Mushrooms

About 8 minutes. Vet review: High.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** Mushrooms for dogs: hype or help?

Scroll pet social media for five minutes and you'll see them: mushroom powders promising calm, focus, immunity and long life. Some labels sound almost magical.

The truth is more interesting, and more modest. A few mushrooms have real research behind them. Many claims run far ahead of the evidence.

Today you'll learn which mushrooms are studied, what the research actually shows, and how to read a label so you don't pay for hype.

**In this lesson:**

- What "functional mushrooms" are

- Reishi, lion's mane, chaga and turkey tail: the evidence

- How to read a mushroom label

- Safety rules

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

Functional mushrooms contain compounds called **beta-glucans** and other molecules that interact with the immune system. Most research is in the lab, in rodents, or in people; studies in dogs are few.

| Mushroom                            | What's studied         | Evidence in dogs                                                              |
|-------------------------------------|------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Turkey tail (*Trametes versicolor*) | Immune support         | One small study in dogs with a type of cancer, alongside vet care (Lesson 25) |
| Reishi (*Ganoderma lucidum*)        | Immune balance, stress | Very limited                                                                  |
| Lion's mane (*Hericium erinaceus*)  | Nerve and brain health | Lab and rodent studies; little in dogs                                        |
| Chaga (*Inonotus obliquus*)         | Antioxidants           | Lab studies; very high in oxalates                                            |

**Fruiting body or mycelium?** The fruiting body is the mushroom you'd see on a tree. Many cheap products are **mycelium grown on grain**, which can be mostly grain starch with fewer beta-glucans. A quality label tells you which part is used and the **beta-glucan content**.

### Screen 3 · Listen — How to read a mushroom label

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> If you decide to try a mushroom supplement, here's what I look for on the label.
>
> One: the Latin name of the mushroom, so you know exactly what's inside.
>
> Two: "fruiting body". If it says "mycelium on grain" or "full spectrum", it may contain a lot of grain filler.
>
> Three: the beta-glucan percentage, ideally tested. Not "polysaccharides", which can include starch.
>
> Four: made for dogs, with a dose by weight, and a batch number.
>
> And five: no big promises. If a label says it treats or cures disease, walk away. In the EU, pet foods and supplements aren't allowed to make those claims.
>
> And never, ever give a dog wild mushrooms you've picked yourself. Some are deadly.

**Check question:** What does a good mushroom label show?

- A promise to cure illness

- **The Latin name, fruiting body, and tested beta-glucan content (correct)**

- Only the word "organic"

- A picture of a mushroom

**Feedback:** Right. Specifics, not slogans.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** What are beta-glucans?

- A type of protein

- **Compounds in mushrooms that interact with the immune system (correct)**

- A kind of vitamin

- A preservative

*Correct:* Yes. They're the main compounds studied in functional mushrooms. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Beta-glucans are immune-active compounds found in mushrooms.

**Q2.** Why can "mycelium on grain" products be weaker?

- Mycelium is toxic

- **They can be mostly grain starch with fewer active compounds (correct)**

- Grain makes dogs itchy

- They're always more expensive

*Correct:* Exactly. Check the beta-glucan content. *Incorrect:* Not quite. The issue is filler starch and fewer beta-glucans.

### Screen 5 · Write — Read a real label

**Practice intro:** Practise on a product you own or one you've seen online.

**Instructions:** Check the five points from the audio and note what's missing.

**Weak example:** "It says immune boost, so it works."

**Why it's weak:** "Immune boost" is marketing, not evidence.

**Placeholder:** Latin name:… / Part used:… / Beta-glucans:… / Dog dose:… / Claims:… / Verdict:…

**Model answer:** "Latin name: Trametes versicolor. Part used: not stated. Beta-glucans: not stated, only 'polysaccharides 30%'. Dog dose: yes, by weight. Claims: 'supports immunity', no cure claims. Verdict: unclear quality; I'd email the company to ask about fruiting body and tested beta-glucans before buying."

**Why it works:** It checks each point and turns gaps into a question for the seller.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** Read a mushroom label.

1.  Choose one mushroom product, at home or online.

2.  Score it against the five checks: Latin name, part used, beta-glucans, dog dose, honest claims.

3.  Save the score in the app.

4.  If you plan to start one, ask your vet first, especially if your dog takes medicines.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Honest expectations

Mushrooms are a "maybe helpful" supplement, not a "must have". They come after the basics: a complete diet, healthy weight, exercise, and dental and parasite care. If those aren't in place, no powder will make up for it.

If you do use one, track something specific (for example energy, stool quality or how your senior dog engages on walks) for 6–8 weeks, so you can judge for yourself.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Talk to your vet before using mushrooms if your dog:**

- Takes immune-suppressing medicine, chemotherapy, blood thinners or diabetes medicine

- Has kidney disease or a history of bladder stones (chaga is high in oxalate)

- Has an autoimmune disease, or is pregnant, nursing or a puppy

**Urgent:** if your dog eats a wild mushroom, call your vet or an animal poison line immediately, even if they seem fine.

**For the next 24 hours:** score one mushroom label.

**Key takeaways:**

- A few mushrooms have promising research, but evidence in dogs is limited.

- Look for fruiting body and tested beta-glucans.

- Never feed wild mushrooms.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Label Sleuth

**Progress:** Lesson 10 of 30 complete · Day 10 streak

**Celebration text:** A third of the course done, and you can spot hype on a label. Next: bone broth, simple and soothing.

## Lesson 11 — Bone Broth

About 7 minutes. Vet review: Standard.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** A warm bowl that says "welcome home"

A cold, wet evening. You pour a little warm broth over dinner and your dog's nose goes straight into the bowl. Even the fussiest eater finishes every bite.

Bone broth is one of the simplest things you can make for your dog. It adds water, flavour and comfort. It isn't a miracle cure, but it's a lovely, practical tool, as long as you make it the dog-safe way.

**In this lesson:**

- What bone broth offers, and what it doesn't

- The two ingredients that must never go in

- A simple dog-safe recipe

- How much to give

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

**What broth offers:**

- **Hydration:** great for dogs who don't drink much, for dry-food eaters, hot days and older dogs.

- **Palatability:** tempts picky or recovering eaters.

- **Gelatin and amino acids** such as glycine and proline, from the collagen in bones, skin and joints.

**What it isn't:** broth is not a complete food and is low in most vitamins and minerals. Claims that it "heals the gut" or "rebuilds joints" are not proven in dogs. Think of it as a tasty, hydrating topper.

**Never add:**

- **Onion, garlic, leeks or chives** (in any form, including powder): they damage dogs' red blood cells.

- **Salt or stock cubes:** shop-bought broths and cubes are often very salty and may contain onion.

### Screen 3 · Listen — Agota's simple broth

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> Here's how I make broth.
>
> Take about a kilo of joint bones: chicken feet, wings, turkey necks or beef knuckles. Put them in a big pot and cover with water. Add a tablespoon of apple cider vinegar. It helps draw out minerals and collagen.
>
> Bring to a gentle simmer, skim the foam, then let it simmer very low for 12 to 24 hours for poultry, or longer for beef. A slow cooker is perfect.
>
> Strain it very carefully. Every bone and bone fragment must come out. Cooked bones splinter and can be dangerous.
>
> Cool it in the fridge. The fat will rise to the top and set; lift most of it off. A good broth turns to jelly when cold. That's the gelatin.
>
> Keep it in the fridge for up to four days, or freeze it in ice-cube trays.

**Check question:** Why must you strain the broth so carefully?

- To make it look nicer

- **Cooked bones can splinter and cause injury (correct)**

- Bones make it salty

- Dogs don't like bits

**Feedback:** Exactly. No cooked bones, ever.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** Which ingredient is safe in dog bone broth?

- Onion

- Garlic

- **Carrot (correct)**

- Stock cubes

*Correct:* Yes. Plain carrot, celery or parsley are fine. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Onion, garlic and stock cubes are unsafe for dogs.

**Q2.** Why skim the fat off cooled broth?

- Fat is toxic

- **Too much fat can upset the stomach and, in some dogs, trigger pancreatitis (correct)**

- It makes the broth too cold

- For colour

*Correct:* Right. Lean broth is safer. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Rich fat can upset the gut and pancreas.

### Screen 5 · Write — Your broth plan

**Practice intro:** Plan your first batch.

**Instructions:** Write the bones you'll use, the safe extras, cooking time, how you'll store it, and the portion for your dog.

**Weak example:** "Leftover roast chicken with gravy, boiled up."

**Why it's weak:** Gravy often contains onion and salt, and roast leftovers can be very fatty.

**Placeholder:** Bones:… / Safe extras:… / Cooking time:… / Storage:… / Portion:…

**Model answer:** "Bones: 1 kg chicken feet and wings. Safe extras: 1 carrot, a handful of parsley, 1 tbsp apple cider vinegar. Cooking time: 18 hours in the slow cooker. Storage: strained, fat removed, frozen in ice-cube trays. Portion: 2 cubes (about 60 ml) on dinner for my 12 kg dog."

**Why it works:** Safe ingredients, clear method, sensible portion.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** Offer broth safely.

1.  Warm a small amount to body temperature (not hot).

2.  Pour about 1 tablespoon per 5 kg body weight over a meal, or offer in a bowl.

3.  Note how your dog likes it and how the stool looks next day.

4.  Count broth within the 10% "extras" allowance for the day.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Smart uses

- **Frozen broth cubes** as a cooling summer treat.

- **Lick-mat topping:** freeze a thin layer of broth on a lick mat for calm enrichment (Lesson 19).

- **Soaking freeze-dried food:** rehydrate freeze-dried meals or toppers with warm broth instead of water for extra appeal.

- **Recovering appetite:** a little warm broth may encourage a dog to eat after a minor upset, but a dog that won't eat for more than a day needs a vet.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Talk to your vet first if your dog:**

- Has pancreatitis, or needs a low-fat diet

- Has kidney or heart disease, or is on a low-sodium or low-protein diet

- Has a chicken or beef allergy (choose a different bone)

**For the next 24 hours:** buy or plan bones for your first batch.

**Key takeaways:**

- Broth adds hydration and flavour; it isn't a complete food.

- Never add onion, garlic, salt or stock cubes.

- Strain out every bone and skim the fat.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Broth Master

**Progress:** Lesson 11 of 30 complete · Day 11 streak

**Celebration text:** You've added a warm, safe treat to your toolkit. Next: superfoods worth a place in the bowl.

## Lesson 12 — Superfoods in the Bowl

About 8 minutes. Vet review: Standard.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** A sprinkle of colour

You're snacking on blueberries. Your dog is watching every one of them travel from bowl to mouth. Can they have one?

Yes, and that little blueberry is a good place to start. A few whole foods add colour, fibre and plant compounds to a dog's bowl. The trick is choosing the right ones, in the right amounts.

**In this lesson:**

- What "superfood" really means for a dog

- Four popular options: blueberries, spirulina, kelp and dandelion

- The 10% rule for toppers

- Foods that look healthy but aren't safe

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

"Superfood" isn't a scientific term. It usually means a food rich in certain nutrients or plant compounds such as **polyphenols** (antioxidants) and fibre.

| Food             | What it offers                               | How to give                                            |
|------------------|----------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|
| Blueberries      | Polyphenols (anthocyanins), fibre, vitamin C | Fresh or frozen, a few as a treat or mashed on food    |
| Spirulina        | Protein, antioxidant pigments, some vitamins | Small pinch; buy tested for heavy metals and toxins    |
| Kelp             | Iodine (often missing in home diets)         | Tiny amounts only; too much iodine harms the thyroid   |
