| Dandelion (leaf) | Fibre, vitamins A and K, potassium           | Small amounts, from unsprayed areas or as a dried herb |

**The 10% rule:** treats, toppers and extras should make up no more than about **10% of daily calories**. The other 90% should be a complete, balanced diet.

### Screen 3 · Listen — Start low, go slow

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> When I add something new to a bowl, I follow three rules.
>
> One new food at a time. Give it for three to five days before adding anything else, so you know how your dog reacts.
>
> Start tiny. A pinch of powder, one or two berries. Then build up slowly.
>
> Watch the poop. Extra fibre can soften the stool, and some greens change the colour. That's often fine, but runny stool means you've gone too fast.
>
> And with kelp, I'm extra careful. It's very rich in iodine, and the amount varies a lot between products. If you cook at home, ask your vet or nutritionist how much your dog's recipe needs, rather than guessing.
>
> Superfoods are the sprinkles, not the cake.

**Check question:** What's Agota's rule for adding new foods?

- Add several at once to save time

- **One new food at a time, starting small (correct)**

- Only on weekends

- Large amounts for faster results

**Feedback:** Right. Slow and simple.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** About how much of daily calories should toppers and treats provide?

- 50%

- 30%

- **Up to about 10% (correct)**

- 0%

*Correct:* Yes. The complete diet does the heavy lifting. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Keep extras to about 10% of calories.

**Q2.** Which of these is toxic to dogs?

- Blueberries

- **Grapes and raisins (correct)**

- Carrots

- Pumpkin

*Correct:* Exactly. Grapes and raisins can cause kidney failure. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Grapes and raisins are the dangerous ones.

### Screen 5 · Write — Choose your topper

**Practice intro:** Pick one superfood to try this week.

**Instructions:** Write which one, why, how much, and how you'll check it suits your dog.

**Weak example:** "I'll add spirulina, kelp, turmeric and berries from tomorrow."

**Why it's weak:** Four new foods at once, no amounts, no way to tell what causes a reaction.

**Placeholder:** Food:… / Why:… / Amount:… / What I'll watch:…

**Model answer:** "Food: blueberries. Why: antioxidants and my dog loves them. Amount: 5 berries a day for my 20 kg dog, mashed on breakfast. What I'll watch: stool score and any itch for 5 days before trying anything else."

**Why it works:** One food, a clear amount, and a plan to observe.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** Add one topper.

1.  Choose one food from the table.

2.  Give the smallest amount today.

3.  Log it in the app.

4.  Check the stool tomorrow and for the next 3–5 days.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Healthy for us, dangerous for dogs

Some "healthy" foods are **toxic** to dogs:

- Grapes, raisins, sultanas and currants

- Onion, garlic, leeks and chives

- Macadamia nuts

- Xylitol (birch sugar), found in some sugar-free peanut butters, gums and baked goods

- Chocolate and cocoa, coffee, and alcohol

- Avocado skin and stone, and cooked bones

Other good toppers: plain cooked pumpkin, steamed broccoli, carrot, green beans, sardines in water, a cooked egg, plain kefir in small amounts.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Talk to your vet first if your dog:**

- Has a thyroid condition (kelp and iodine)

- Has kidney disease or a history of bladder stones (some greens are high in oxalate or potassium)

- Takes blood thinners or diabetes medicine

**Urgent:** if your dog eats grapes, raisins, xylitol or another toxic food, call your vet or an animal poison line straight away.

**For the next 24 hours:** add one topper and log it.

**Key takeaways:**

- Superfoods are sprinkles, not the main meal.

- One new food at a time, starting small.

- Keep extras to about 10% of calories.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Colourful Bowl

**Progress:** Lesson 12 of 30 complete · Day 12 streak

**Celebration text:** Your dog's bowl just got brighter, and safer. Next: supporting joints for an active life.

## Lesson 13 — Joint Support & Arthritis

About 9 minutes. Vet review: High.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** The slow morning stretch

Your dog used to leap off the sofa. Now there's a pause before getting up, a slow stretch, a stiff first few steps on cold mornings. You tell yourself it's just age.

Often it's **osteoarthritis**, and it's very common: many dogs over eight show signs, and some much younger. The good news is there's a lot you can do to keep your dog moving comfortably.

**In this lesson:**

- What osteoarthritis is

- The quiet signs owners miss

- The single most powerful thing you can do

- Which joint supplements have evidence

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

In a healthy joint, smooth cartilage and joint fluid let bones glide. In osteoarthritis, cartilage wears down, the joint becomes inflamed, and movement hurts. It can't be reversed, but it can be managed.

**The quiet signs:**

- Slow to get up, stiff after rest

- Hesitates at stairs, the car or the sofa

- Shorter walks, lagging behind, sitting down on walks

- Licking a joint, grumpy when touched

- Less play, more sleep

**What helps most, in order of evidence:**

1.  **Healthy body weight.** In a landmark study, Labradors kept lean lived about two years longer and developed arthritis later than their heavier siblings. Even modest weight loss can noticeably improve lameness in overweight dogs.

2.  **Regular, gentle exercise:** daily walks, swimming, avoiding sudden bursts.

3.  **Omega-3s (EPA and DHA):** the joint supplement with the strongest evidence in dogs.

4.  **Vet pain relief and other therapies** when needed.

### Screen 3 · Listen — Supplements, honestly

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> The shelves are full of joint supplements. Here's my honest view.
>
> Omega-3s from fish or green-lipped mussel have the best evidence for dogs with arthritis. Several studies showed better mobility with omega-3-rich diets.
>
> Glucosamine and chondroitin are very popular. The evidence is mixed. Some dogs seem to improve, studies don't agree, and it may take six to eight weeks to see any change.
>
> Collagen products are newer. There are a few early studies, which we'll look at in the next lesson.
>
> What none of them can do is replace weight control, sensible exercise and a vet's pain plan. A dog in pain doesn't just need a powder. Arthritis pain is real pain, and modern vet medicine has good, safe options.
>
> So: weight first, movement second, omega-3s third, and your vet throughout.

**Check question:** Which joint supplement has the strongest evidence in dogs?

- Turmeric

- **Omega-3s (EPA and DHA) (correct)**

- Apple cider vinegar

- Calcium

**Feedback:** Exactly. Omega-3s lead the evidence.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** What's the single most powerful thing for arthritic joints?

- An expensive supplement

- Complete rest

- **Keeping the dog at a healthy, lean weight (correct)**

- A soft bed only

*Correct:* Yes. Less weight means less load on every joint. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Weight control has the biggest proven effect.

**Q2.** Which exercise is usually best for arthritic dogs?

- Weekend-only long hikes

- Ball chasing with sharp turns

- **Regular, steady walks and swimming (correct)**

- No exercise at all

*Correct:* Right. Little and often keeps joints moving. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Steady, regular, low-impact exercise is best.

### Screen 5 · Write — Your mobility notes

**Practice intro:** Describe what you see, so you can track changes.

**Instructions:** Note how your dog gets up, uses stairs, walks and plays.

**Weak example:** "She's getting old."

**Why it's weak:** It accepts pain as normal and gives nothing to track.

**Placeholder:** Getting up:… / Stairs and car:… / Walks:… / Play:… / Other:…

**Model answer:** "Getting up: slow and stiff after naps, better after 5 minutes. Stairs and car: hesitates at the car, needs a lift. Walks: 40 min a year ago, now sits down after 25. Play: drops the ball after 2 throws. Other: licks right elbow at night. Book a vet check."

**Why it works:** It's specific, comparable over time, and leads to action.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** The mobility check.

1.  Watch your dog get up after a nap. Score 0 (easy) to 3 (very stiff).

2.  Watch them climb 3–4 stairs or jump into the car. Score 0–3.

3.  Film 10 seconds of walking on a flat surface. Look for limping, head bobbing or uneven steps.

4.  Save the scores and the video. Repeat monthly, and share them with your vet.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Around the home

- **Rugs and runners** on slippery floors.

- **Ramps or steps** for the car and sofa.

- **A firm, supportive bed** away from draughts.

- **Raised bowls** can help some dogs with neck or front-leg pain.

- **Nail trims:** long nails change how a dog stands.

- **Warm-up:** a gentle 5-minute walk before running.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**See your vet if:**

- Your dog limps for more than a day or two, or won't put weight on a leg (go the same day if severe or after an injury)

- There's swelling, heat, or crying out when touched

- Stiffness is getting worse, or your dog is under five years old and stiff

- You want to start a supplement alongside medicines

**Never give human painkillers** such as ibuprofen, paracetamol or aspirin unless your vet tells you to. Some are toxic to dogs.

**For the next 24 hours:** do the mobility check and save the video.

**Key takeaways:**

- Stiffness isn't "just age"; it's often treatable pain.

- Lean weight and steady exercise matter most.

- Omega-3s have the best supplement evidence.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Joint Guardian

**Progress:** Lesson 13 of 30 complete · Day 13 streak

**Celebration text:** You've learned to spot quiet pain and support comfortable movement. Next: collagen and hyaluronic acid.

## Lesson 14 — Collagen & Hyaluronic Acid

About 7 minutes. Vet review: Standard.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** The body's glue and cushion

You've probably seen collagen powders in your own kitchen or your friend's. Now there are versions for dogs too, with hyaluronic acid promising "juicy joints".

What are these substances, what does the research say, and can food supply them naturally? Let's find out.

**In this lesson:**

- What collagen and hyaluronic acid do in the body

- Food sources that contain them

- What studies in dogs show

- How to decide if a product is worth it

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

**Collagen** is the most abundant protein in the body. It gives structure to skin, tendons, ligaments, cartilage and bone. It's rich in the amino acids glycine, proline and hydroxyproline.

**Hyaluronic acid (HA)** is a natural substance in joint fluid and cartilage. It holds water and helps joint fluid stay thick and slippery, like a cushion and lubricant.

**Food sources:** skin, cartilage, tendons and connective tissue: chicken feet, turkey necks, beef trachea, pig ears, fish skin, and broth made from them (Lesson 11). Rooster combs are a traditional HA source.

**Does eating collagen go straight to the joints?** Not directly. It's digested into small pieces (peptides and amino acids). Some research suggests certain peptides may signal the body to support cartilage, but this is still being studied.

**Evidence in dogs:** a few small studies on collagen peptides and on undenatured type II collagen (UC-II) reported improved mobility scores in dogs with arthritis. The studies are small, and some were funded by manufacturers. Evidence for HA by mouth in dogs is limited.

### Screen 3 · Listen — Food first

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> I like to start with food before powders.
>
> If your dog eats raw or fresh food, connective tissue like chicken feet, necks or trachea naturally adds collagen. If you cook at home, a gelatin-rich bone broth is a gentle way to add it.
>
> If you choose a supplement, I'd look for three things. A product made for dogs, with a clear dose by weight. The type of collagen named, such as hydrolysed peptides or undenatured type two. And a company that shares its testing.
>
> Then give it a fair trial: about eight weeks, while you track mobility with the check from Lesson 13. If you see no difference after that, it may not be worth the money for your dog.
>
> And remember, collagen goes alongside the basics: a lean weight, steady exercise, omega-3s and your vet's plan.

**Check question:** How long should you trial a joint supplement before judging it?

- 2 days

- 1 week

- **About 8 weeks (correct)**

- 1 year

**Feedback:** Right. Joints change slowly.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** What does hyaluronic acid do in the joint?

- Builds muscle

- **Helps keep joint fluid thick and slippery (correct)**

- Strengthens teeth

- Burns fat

*Correct:* Yes. It's a natural lubricant and cushion. *Incorrect:* Not quite. HA helps joint fluid stay thick and slippery.

**Q2.** Which food is naturally rich in collagen?

- Rice

- Apples

- **Chicken feet (correct)**

- Lean chicken breast

*Correct:* Exactly. Connective tissue is where collagen lives. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Look for skin, cartilage and connective tissue like chicken feet.

### Screen 5 · Write — Food or supplement?

**Practice intro:** Decide how you'd add collagen, if at all.

**Instructions:** Write your dog's current collagen sources, what you'd add, and how you'd measure the result.

**Weak example:** "I'll buy the most expensive joint powder."

**Why it's weak:** Price doesn't equal evidence, and there's no way to judge it.

**Placeholder:** Current sources:… / What I'd add:… / How I'll measure:…

**Model answer:** "Current sources: none, she eats cooked turkey and rice with a balancer. What I'd add: 60 ml homemade chicken-foot broth three times a week. How I'll measure: mobility check scores now and in 8 weeks."

**Why it works:** Food-first, affordable, and measurable.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** Pick one collagen-rich addition that suits your feeding style and note it in the app with today's date. Repeat the mobility check from Lesson 13 in 8 weeks.

If your dog is a strong chewer, choose chews that are the right size and always supervise.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Chews and safety

Collagen-rich chews such as trachea, pig ears and fish skins can also help keep teeth clean and give mental enrichment. But they carry calories (count them within the 10% treat allowance), and some can be fatty. Always supervise, choose a chew larger than your dog's mouth, and take it away when it becomes small enough to swallow. Rawhide pieces and cooked bones are best avoided.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Talk to your vet if your dog:**

- Has kidney disease (protein and some chews may need limiting)

- Has pancreatitis or needs a low-fat diet (some chews are fatty)

- Has a known food allergy (collagen products come from specific animals)

- Is limping or in pain: supplements don't replace a check-up

**For the next 24 hours:** choose and log one collagen-rich addition.

**Key takeaways:**

- Collagen gives structure; hyaluronic acid cushions joints.

- Food sources come first; supplements have small, early evidence.

- Trial for 8 weeks and measure.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Joint Builder

**Progress:** Lesson 14 of 30 complete · Day 14 streak

**Celebration text:** Two full weeks! You're building real expertise. Next: safe, steady weight loss.

## Lesson 15 — Safe Weight Loss

About 9 minutes. Vet review: High.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** Love isn't measured in treats

A piece of cheese for sitting nicely. A crust from your toast. A biscuit because those eyes are impossible to resist. Every one of them is love, and together they add up.

Across Europe, many vets estimate that around half of pet dogs are overweight. Extra weight is linked to arthritis, breathing problems, diabetes and a shorter life. And many owners don't see it, because a chubby dog has become "normal".

Today you'll learn to see your dog's shape honestly and plan a safe, steady weight loss.

**In this lesson:**

- The body condition score (BCS)

- How fast is safe

- How to cut calories without hunger

- Tracking progress

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

**Body condition score (1–9):** vets use a 9-point scale.

| Score          | What you see and feel                                                                          |
|----------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1–3 Too thin   | Ribs, spine and hip bones easy to see, little or no fat                                        |
| 4–5 Ideal      | Ribs easy to feel with a thin fat layer; clear waist from above; belly tucked up from the side |
| 6–7 Overweight | Ribs hard to feel; waist barely visible; belly not tucked                                      |
| 8–9 Obese      | Ribs very hard or impossible to feel; no waist; fat on back and tail base                      |

Each point above 5 is roughly **10% extra body weight**.

**Safe pace:** about **1–2% of body weight per week**, under vet guidance. Faster loss can cost muscle, and a dog that's very hungry is more likely to scavenge.

**How:** feed for the **target weight**, not the current weight, weigh food with a kitchen scale, and count every treat.

### Screen 3 · Listen — Less food, not less love

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> Cutting food can feel unkind. So here are ways to keep your dog happy while the weight comes off.
>
> Weigh every meal. A scoop or cup can be out by 20% or more.
>
> Use part of the daily food as treats. Take a handful from breakfast and use it for training.
>
> Choose low-calorie extras: green beans, cucumber, carrot, a few blueberries.
>
> Add volume, not calories. Fresh and lightly cooked foods hold more water than dry food, and a spoon of steamed vegetables helps fill the bowl.
>
> Make food last. Lick mats, snuffle mats and puzzle feeders turn a meal into an activity.
>
> And talk to the family. One person giving secret snacks can undo everything.
>
> Love is a walk, a game, a brush, time together. Not a biscuit.

**Check question:** How can you reward your dog without extra calories?

- Give larger biscuits

- **Use part of their daily food as training treats (correct)**

- Skip meals and give treats instead

- Share your dinner

**Feedback:** Exactly. Same food, more fun.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** What's a safe rate of weight loss for most dogs?

- 10% per week

- **About 1–2% of body weight per week (correct)**

- As fast as possible

- 5 kg per month regardless of size

*Correct:* Yes. Slow and steady protects muscle. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Aim for about 1–2% a week with your vet.

**Q2.** On a 1–9 scale, what's the ideal body condition score?

- 1–2

- **4–5 (correct)**

- 7

- 9

*Correct:* Right. Ribs easy to feel, visible waist. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Ideal is 4–5.

### Screen 5 · Write — Your weight plan

**Practice intro:** Write a realistic plan.

**Instructions:** Note current weight, BCS, target weight (agreed with your vet), weekly target, and three changes you'll make.

**Weak example:** "Feed less and walk more."

**Why it's weak:** No numbers, no plan, nothing to track.

**Placeholder:** Current weight:… / BCS:… / Target:… / Weekly goal:… / Changes:…

**Model answer:** "Current weight: 34 kg. BCS: 7. Target: about 29 kg (agreed with vet). Weekly goal: 0.3–0.6 kg. Changes: 1) weigh all food; 2) 20 g of breakfast kept for training; 3) no table scraps, family agreed. Weigh every 2 weeks."

**Why it works:** Specific, safe and trackable.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** The body condition score.

1.  **Ribs:** run your flat hands along the sides. Can you feel each rib easily with a light layer over them, like the back of your hand?

2.  **Waist:** look from above. Is there an hourglass?

3.  **Tummy:** look from the side. Does the belly tuck up behind the ribs?

4.  Score 1–9 using the table, weigh your dog, and save both in the app with a photo from above and from the side.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — When the scale won't move

If weight doesn't change after 4 weeks of careful feeding, check for hidden calories (chews, dental sticks, family snacks), weigh food again, and see your vet. Some medical conditions, such as an underactive thyroid, can make weight loss hard. Vets also have prescription weight-loss diets that are high in protein and fibre, which help preserve muscle and keep dogs full.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Start weight loss with your vet if your dog:**

- Has a BCS of 7 or more, or has gained weight fast

- Has other conditions (joint, heart, breathing, diabetes)

- Is drinking or peeing more, very tired, or losing hair (possible hormone problems)

- Is a puppy, pregnant or nursing (never put them on a weight-loss diet without a vet)

**For the next 24 hours:** score your dog's BCS and weigh them.

**Key takeaways:**

- Ideal BCS is 4–5; ribs easy to feel, visible waist.

- Lose about 1–2% a week, with your vet.

- Weigh food and count every treat.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Healthy Shape

**Progress:** Lesson 15 of 30 complete · Day 15 streak

**Celebration text:** Halfway there! You've learned one of the most powerful health skills of all. Next: the other side, helping a thin dog gain healthily.

## Lesson 16 — Healthy Weight Gain

About 8 minutes. Vet review: High.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** When the ribs show too much

You stroke your dog's back and feel every bump of the spine. People at the park ask if they're eating enough. You fill the bowl, but they walk away half-finished.

Some dogs are naturally lean, and some breeds look slim. But a dog that is too thin, or losing weight without trying, needs attention. Today you'll learn to tell the difference and how to add weight the healthy way.

**In this lesson:**

- Thin by nature vs. too thin

- Muscle condition, not just fat

- Why weight loss without trying needs a vet

- Healthy ways to add weight

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

**Body condition score** (Lesson 15) shows fat. A score of **3 or below** is too thin.

**Muscle condition score** shows muscle, which matters even more in older or ill dogs. Vets check the muscle over the spine, shoulder blades, skull and hips: normal, mild, moderate or severe loss. A dog can be overweight and still losing muscle.

**Common causes of being underweight:**

- Not enough calories for activity (working and sport dogs, cold weather)

- Picky eating, or a food that isn't appealing

- Dental pain

- Worms and other parasites

- Stress, a new home, competition with other pets

- Medical problems: digestive, thyroid, kidney, liver, diabetes, cancer

**Unplanned weight loss of more than 5–10% is a reason to see your vet.**

### Screen 3 · Listen — Calories that count

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> Once your vet has ruled out illness, here's how I help a dog gain healthily.
>
> First, feed more meals. Three or four smaller meals are easier to eat than one big one.
>
> Second, choose more energy-dense food. Fresh, raw and freeze-dried foods are often very tasty and rich in protein and fat. A complete food made for active dogs, or a puppy food recommended by your vet, can also help.
>
> Third, make it smell wonderful. Warm the food slightly, add a spoon of bone broth, or crumble a freeze-dried topper on top.
>
> Fourth, add healthy extras, like a cooked egg, sardines in water, or a little extra meat, within a balanced diet.
>
> And build muscle, not just fat: gentle walks, hill climbs and sniffing games.
>
> Aim for slow gain, around 1 to 2 percent a week, and weigh every week.

**Check question:** What's the first step before trying to add weight?

- Double the food immediately

- Add lots of oil

- **See the vet to rule out illness (correct)**

- Feed only treats

**Feedback:** Right. Rule out the causes first.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** What does the muscle condition score measure?

- Fat under the skin

- **Muscle over the spine, shoulders, skull and hips (correct)**

- Coat quality

- Height

*Correct:* Yes. Muscle tells you about strength and health. *Incorrect:* Not quite. MCS checks muscle over key areas.

**Q2.** Which change usually helps a thin dog eat more?

- One large meal a day

- **Smaller, more frequent meals that smell appealing (correct)**

- Cold food straight from the fridge

- Fewer walks

*Correct:* Exactly. More meals, more appeal. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Smaller, more frequent, tastier meals help most.

### Screen 5 · Write — Your gain plan

**Practice intro:** Plan a steady gain.

**Instructions:** Write current weight, BCS, MCS, the vet's advice, and your changes.

**Weak example:** "I'll give him more treats."

**Why it's weak:** Treats aren't balanced and can make a picky dog pickier.

**Placeholder:** Weight:… / BCS:… / MCS:… / Vet advice:… / Changes:…

**Model answer:** "Weight: 18 kg. BCS: 3. MCS: mild loss over the spine. Vet: blood tests normal, teeth fine, wormed. Changes: 3 meals instead of 2; warm the food with broth; add a cooked egg 3 times a week; daily hill walk. Weigh every Sunday."

**Why it works:** Medical causes ruled out, practical changes, regular weighing.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** The muscle condition check.

1.  Run your fingers along the spine. Can you feel muscle on both sides, or sharp bones?

2.  Feel the shoulder blades and hips. Firm muscle or bony?

3.  Gently feel the top of the skull. Any hollowing at the temples?

4.  Note "normal", "mild", "moderate" or "severe" loss, weigh your dog, and save it with a photo.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Picky or poorly?

A picky eater that is otherwise bright, active and keeping weight is often a dog that has learned that refusing food brings something better. Offer a meal for 15–20 minutes, then remove it until the next meal, and avoid swapping foods every day.

A dog that used to eat well and has gone off food, is drinking more, vomiting, has diarrhoea, or seems tired is not picky. That's a vet visit.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**See your vet if your dog:**

- Has lost weight without trying, or has a BCS of 3 or lower

- Has muscle loss, especially over the head or spine

- Eats a lot but still loses weight

- Is off food for more than 24 hours (sooner for puppies, seniors or small dogs), vomiting, has diarrhoea, or is drinking much more

- Has bad breath, drooling or chews on one side (dental pain)

**For the next 24 hours:** do the muscle condition check and weigh your dog.

**Key takeaways:**

- Rule out illness before adding weight.

- Check muscle as well as fat.

- More, smaller, tastier meals and gentle exercise.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Strength Builder

**Progress:** Lesson 16 of 30 complete · Day 16 streak

**Celebration text:** You can now read both fat and muscle. Next: blood sugar and the early signs of diabetes.

## Lesson 17 — Blood Sugar & Diabetes

About 9 minutes. Vet review: Critical.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** The water bowl that's always empty

You fill the water bowl in the morning. By lunchtime it's empty. Your dog asks to go out at night, which they never used to. They're eating well, maybe more than ever, yet they look thinner.

These can be early signs of **diabetes mellitus**. Caught early and managed with your vet, many diabetic dogs live full, happy lives. Today you'll learn the signs, and how food fits in.

**In this lesson:**

- What diabetes is in dogs

- The early signs

- How diet supports treatment

- Who is at higher risk

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

After a meal, sugar (glucose) enters the blood. The hormone **insulin**, made by the pancreas, moves it into cells for energy. In most diabetic dogs, the pancreas makes too little insulin. This is similar to type 1 diabetes in people, so **almost all diabetic dogs need insulin injections for life**. Diet supports insulin; it can't replace it.

**Early signs:**

- Drinking much more (polydipsia)

- Peeing much more, accidents indoors (polyuria)

- Increased appetite, yet weight loss

- Tiredness

- Later: cloudy eyes (cataracts), repeated infections

**Higher risk:** middle-aged and older dogs, unspayed females, overweight dogs, dogs with past pancreatitis, and some breeds such as Samoyeds, Miniature Schnauzers, Poodles, Australian Terriers and Beagles. Some medicines (for example long-term steroids) can raise the risk.

### Screen 3 · Listen — Food and insulin, a team

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> If your dog has diabetes, food and insulin work as a team. Your vet will build the plan, but here are the principles.
>
> Consistency is everything. The same food, the same amount, at the same times every day, usually twice a day, with insulin given around each meal as your vet directs.
>
> Don't change the food suddenly. A new food changes how much sugar enters the blood, so any change should be planned with your vet.
>
> Fibre, protein and fat matter. Many vets use diets with more fibre and moderate fat, or lower carbohydrate, to make blood sugar steadier. The right balance depends on your dog.
>
> Treats need planning too. Choose low-sugar ones, at the same times each day, and count them.
>
> And keep a healthy weight. It makes insulin work better.
>
> Never adjust or stop insulin yourself. Too much insulin can cause dangerously low blood sugar.

**Check question:** What's most important when feeding a diabetic dog?

- Changing foods often for variety

- Feeding whenever the dog is hungry

- **The same food and amount at the same times, as the vet plans (correct)**

- Lots of fruit for energy

**Feedback:** Exactly. Consistency keeps blood sugar steady.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** Which pair of signs is classic for diabetes?

- Itchy ears and sneezing

- **Drinking and peeing much more (correct)**

- Limping and stiffness

- Shedding and dandruff

*Correct:* Yes. Thirst and peeing are key early signs. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Watch for increased thirst and urination.

**Q2.** Can diet alone treat diabetes in most dogs?

- Yes, always

- **No, most diabetic dogs need insulin; diet supports it (correct)**

- Only raw diets can

- Only if you fast the dog

*Correct:* Right. Diet is a partner to insulin, not a replacement. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Most diabetic dogs need insulin for life.

### Screen 5 · Write — The water test

**Practice intro:** Measuring is better than guessing.

**Instructions:** Measure how much your dog drinks in 24 hours, and write it down with their weight.

**Weak example:** "He drinks a lot, I think."

**Why it's weak:** "A lot" can't be compared or shared with a vet.

**Placeholder:** Water at start:… / Water added:… / Left after 24 h:… / Total drunk:… / Dog's weight:… / ml per kg:…

**Model answer:** "Start: 1500 ml. Added: 500 ml. Left after 24 h: 400 ml. Total: 1600 ml. Weight: 16 kg. That's 100 ml per kg. Above the usual guide, so I'll book a vet visit and bring a urine sample."

**Why it works:** Real numbers, a clear comparison, and action.

**Guide:** most healthy dogs drink about 50–60 ml per kg per day. More than **100 ml per kg** is considered excessive and needs a vet check. Dogs on fresh, raw or wet food usually drink less from the bowl because their food contains water. Hot weather and exercise increase thirst.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** Know the early signs.

1.  Measure water drunk over 24 hours (above).

2.  Note any accidents, night-time toilet trips, or bigger urine puddles.

3.  Weigh your dog and compare with last month.

4.  Save it in the app. If any sign is new, book a vet check.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Low blood sugar (hypoglycaemia)

If a diabetic dog gets too much insulin, misses a meal, or exercises much more than usual, blood sugar can drop dangerously. Signs: weakness, wobbliness, trembling, confusion, seizures.

Your vet will give you an emergency plan. It often includes rubbing a little honey or glucose syrup on the gums and **calling the vet immediately**. Never give insulin to a dog that hasn't eaten unless your vet has told you to.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**See your vet promptly if your dog:**

- Drinks or pees much more than usual, or has new accidents

- Eats more but loses weight

- Has sudden cloudy eyes

**Emergency (go now):** vomiting, not eating, very tired or weak, sweet-smelling breath, fast breathing, or collapse in a dog with known or suspected diabetes. These can be signs of diabetic ketoacidosis.

**For the next 24 hours:** do the water test.

**Key takeaways:**

- Thirst, peeing, hunger and weight loss are early signs.

- Most diabetic dogs need insulin; diet supports it.

- Consistency in food and timing is key.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Early Spotter

**Progress:** Lesson 17 of 30 complete · Day 17 streak

**Celebration text:** You know the signs that matter most. Next: the fascinating link between the gut and the brain.

## Lesson 18 — The Gut-Brain Axis

About 8 minutes. Vet review: High.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** A nervous tummy

The car comes out, the suitcase appears, or there's a thunderstorm. A few hours later, your dog has an upset stomach. Or the other way round: a week of tummy trouble, and your normally cheerful dog seems flat and clingy.

That's not a coincidence. The gut and brain are in constant conversation. Today you'll learn how they talk and what you can do to support both.

**In this lesson:**

- What the gut-brain axis is

- How stress affects digestion, and digestion affects mood

- What research in dogs suggests

- A simple mood and food diary

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

The gut and brain are linked by:

- **The vagus nerve:** a direct line of messages in both directions.

- **Hormones:** stress hormones like cortisol change gut movement and the gut lining.

- **The immune system:** much of it sits in the gut wall.

- **Gut microbes:** they produce substances, such as short-chain fatty acids, that can influence the nervous system. The gut also makes most of the body's serotonin, though this mostly acts locally in the gut.

**In dogs:** studies have found differences in the gut microbiome between anxious or aggressive dogs and calm dogs, and a few trials of specific probiotic strains (for example *Bifidobacterium longum* BL999) reported calmer behaviour in anxious dogs. This is a young, exciting field, but it isn't yet proof that changing food or probiotics will change behaviour.

**What's clearer:** stress commonly causes digestive upsets, especially "stress diarrhoea".

### Screen 3 · Listen — Calm gut, calm dog

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> Here's what I'd do to support both gut and mood.
>
> Keep the routine predictable. Meals, walks and sleep at roughly the same times. Dogs feel safer when they know what comes next.
>
> Feed the gut microbes. A complete diet with some fibre from vegetables, and fermentable fibres like those in Lesson 3, supports a healthy microbiome.
>
> Plan ahead for stress. Before a known stressful event, such as a trip, fireworks or visitors, keep food the same and avoid new treats. Some vets suggest a probiotic for a few days before and after.
>
> Use calming activities. Sniffing, licking and chewing help many dogs settle. We'll look at this in the next lesson.
>
> And if your dog's behaviour changes suddenly, think health first. Pain or illness often shows up as a mood change.

**Check question:** What should you think of first when a dog's behaviour changes suddenly?

- Change the food

- Ignore it

- **A health problem, like pain or illness (correct)**

- More exercise

**Feedback:** Right. Health first, always.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** What is the vagus nerve's role in the gut-brain axis?

- It digests food

- **It carries messages between gut and brain in both directions (correct)**

- It makes insulin

- It controls the bladder

*Correct:* Yes. It's a key communication line. *Incorrect:* Not quite. The vagus nerve links gut and brain.

**Q2.** What's a common gut sign of stress in dogs?

- Shinier coat

- **Loose stools after a stressful event (correct)**

- Better appetite

- Smaller ears

*Correct:* Exactly. Stress diarrhoea is common. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Loose stools after stress are very common.

### Screen 5 · Write — Mood and food diary

**Practice intro:** Patterns appear when you write them down.

**Instructions:** For one day, note food, stool, mood and any stressful events.

**Weak example:** "Normal day."

**Why it's weak:** Nothing to compare later.

**Placeholder:** Meals:… / Stool score:… / Mood (1–5):… / Stress events:… / Notes:…

**Model answer:** "Meals: 8:00 and 18:00, usual fresh food. Stool: 2 (firm). Mood: 4, relaxed. Stress events: delivery van at 14:00, barked and paced for 10 minutes. Notes: settled with a lick mat; stool tomorrow?"

**Why it works:** It links events, mood and digestion, building a useful record.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** Start a 7-day mood and food diary.

1.  Log meals and treats.

2.  Score stool (Lesson 1) and mood from 1 (very stressed) to 5 (very relaxed).

3.  Note any stressful events.

4.  After 7 days, look for patterns, and share them with your vet or trainer if needed.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Fibre and calm

When gut microbes ferment fibre, they produce short-chain fatty acids such as butyrate. These feed the cells of the gut lining and are being studied for their effects on inflammation and the nervous system. For dogs, this is one more reason to include some well-tolerated fibre, from cooked pumpkin, sweet potato, green beans or a prebiotic, in a complete diet. Introduce it slowly to avoid gas.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**See your vet if your dog:**

- Suddenly becomes anxious, aggressive, withdrawn or clingy

- Has repeated stress diarrhoea, or diarrhoea lasting more than 48 hours

- Has anxiety that affects daily life (your vet may refer you to a behaviourist)

**For the next 24 hours:** start the mood and food diary.

**Key takeaways:**

- The gut and brain talk constantly.

- Stress affects digestion; gut health may affect mood.

- Routine, fibre and calm activities support both.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Mind-Gut Explorer

**Progress:** Lesson 18 of 30 complete · Day 18 streak

**Celebration text:** You're seeing your dog as a whole, body and mind. Next: turning mealtimes into brain work.

## Lesson 19 — Feeding as Brain Work

About 7 minutes. Vet review: Standard.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** Dinner in 30 seconds flat

You put the bowl down. You turn to wash a cup. When you turn back, the bowl is spotless and your dog is looking at you as if to say, "Is that it?"

Wild dogs spend hours finding, chasing and working for food. Our dogs get it in seconds. That leaves a lot of brain power, and energy, with nowhere to go.

Today you'll learn how to turn mealtimes into enrichment that calms, satisfies and tires your dog in a good way.

**In this lesson:**

- Why food work is good for dogs

- Lick mats, KONGs, snuffle mats and slow feeders

- How to fit them to any feeding style

- Hygiene and safety

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

**Why it works:**

- **Licking** releases feel-good brain chemicals and helps many dogs settle.

- **Sniffing** is mentally tiring. A short sniffing session can tire a dog as much as a longer walk.

- **Chewing** relieves stress and keeps jaws busy.

- **Slower eating** may reduce gulping, and for deep-chested breeds, gulping air is one of the factors linked to bloat.

**The toolkit:**

| Tool                  | Best for                      | Ideas                                                |
|-----------------------|-------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|
| Lick mat              | Calming, grooming, vet visits | Spread wet, fresh or rehydrated food, kefir, pumpkin |
| KONG or stuffable toy | Longer focus, alone time      | Pack with fresh food or soaked kibble, freeze it     |
| Snuffle mat           | Sniffing, speed eaters        | Hide dry or freeze-dried pieces in the fabric        |
| Slow feeder bowl      | Every meal, speed eaters      | Fits most food types                                 |
| Scatter feeding       | Garden sniffing games         | Scatter freeze-dried bits or dry food on grass       |

### Screen 3 · Listen — Start easy

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> The secret with food puzzles is to make them easy at first.
>
> If your dog has never used a KONG, fill it loosely so food falls out with a nudge. Once they get it, pack it tighter. Then try freezing it.
>
> For a lick mat, start with a thin layer of something soft. Fresh or raw food works brilliantly, and freeze-dried food can be soaked and spread.
>
> For a snuffle mat, sprinkle food on top first, then start hiding it deeper.
>
> Always use part of the daily food allowance, so you're not adding calories.
>
> And watch your dog the first few times. Some dogs try to chew or swallow the toy. Choose the right size and a tough material.
>
> When your dog finishes a puzzle and lies down with a big sigh, that's the sound of a satisfied brain.

**Check question:** How should you start a dog on a new food puzzle?

- Make it as hard as possible

- **Make it easy at first, then build difficulty (correct)**

- Only use treats

- Leave the dog alone with it

**Feedback:** Exactly. Success first, challenge later.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** Why can licking help dogs settle?

- It cleans teeth

- **It releases feel-good brain chemicals and is soothing (correct)**

- It burns many calories

- It replaces walks

*Correct:* Yes. Licking is naturally calming. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Licking soothes and relaxes.

**Q2.** How do you avoid extra calories with enrichment?

- Only use human food

- **Use part of the daily food allowance (correct)**

- Give enrichment only once a month

- Use high-calorie treats

*Correct:* Right. Same food, more fun. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Take enrichment food from the daily portion.

### Screen 5 · Write — Your enrichment week

**Practice intro:** Plan a week of brain-work meals.

**Instructions:** Choose one tool for each day and what goes in it.

**Weak example:** "I'll buy some toys."

**Why it's weak:** No plan, no food, no routine.

**Placeholder:** Mon:… / Tue:… / Wed:… / Thu:… / Fri:… / Sat:… / Sun:…

**Model answer:** "Mon: breakfast in slow feeder. Tue: dinner on a lick mat. Wed: frozen KONG with fresh food before work. Thu: snuffle mat with freeze-dried pieces. Fri: scatter feed in the garden. Sat: lick mat during grooming. Sun: frozen KONG after the long walk."

**Why it works:** Varied, realistic and built from the daily food.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

Today, serve one meal (or part of a meal) using a lick mat, KONG, snuffle mat, slow feeder, or a simple scatter. No equipment? Use a muffin tin with food under tennis balls, or a rolled towel with food inside. Time how long it takes and note how your dog seems afterwards.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Hygiene and safety

- **Wash** lick mats, KONGs and slow feeders after every use, especially with raw or fresh food. Many are dishwasher-safe.

- **Check for damage** and replace chewed toys.

- **Supervise** with new toys and strong chewers.

- **Multi-dog homes:** feed puzzles separately to avoid guarding and fights.

- **Puppies:** use puppy-sized, soft toys and their own food.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Talk to your vet if your dog:**

- Gulps food so fast they choke, cough or vomit

- Is a deep-chested breed and you're worried about bloat

- Guards food or toys, or growls at people near the bowl (also consider a qualified behaviourist)

**Emergency:** if your dog swallows part of a toy, or has a swollen hard belly with retching that brings nothing up, see a vet immediately.

**For the next 24 hours:** serve one meal as brain work.

**Key takeaways:**

- Licking, sniffing and chewing calm and tire dogs.

- Start easy, then build difficulty.

- Use daily food, and keep it clean and safe.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Puzzle Pro

**Progress:** Lesson 19 of 30 complete · Day 19 streak

**Celebration text:** Mealtimes just became playtime for the brain. Next: building strong foundations in puppies.

## Lesson 20 — Puppy Nutrition

About 10 minutes. Vet review: Critical.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** Building a body from scratch

A puppy can grow many times their birth weight in their first year. Every bone, muscle, tooth and nerve is being built, and it's being built from what goes in the bowl.

Mistakes in this year can last a lifetime, especially in large breeds. The good news: the rules are clear, and you'll learn them today.

**In this lesson:**

- Why puppies need different food

- The calcium balance, and why too much is as bad as too little

- Large breeds: growing slowly on purpose

- Feeding fresh, home-cooked or raw to puppies safely

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

Puppies need more energy, protein, calcium, phosphorus and certain nutrients (like DHA) per kilogram than adults. Their food must be labelled **complete for growth** (or "all life stages").

**The calcium balance:**

- Calcium and phosphorus must be in the right amounts and ratio (roughly 1:1 to 1.8:1 calcium to phosphorus).

- **Too little calcium** (for example a meat-only home diet) can cause weak, bent or broken bones.

- **Too much calcium** (for example adding supplements to a complete puppy food) can disturb bone development. **Large-breed puppies are especially sensitive**, as they can't regulate calcium absorption well when young.

**Large breeds** (expected adult weight over about 25 kg): need a food formulated for large-breed growth, with controlled calcium and energy. Growing too fast increases the risk of joint problems such as hip and elbow dysplasia. Aim for slow, steady growth and a lean body.

**Never add calcium or bone meal to a complete puppy food.**

### Screen 3 · Listen — Home and raw for puppies

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> Many families want to feed their puppy fresh, home-cooked or raw food. It can be done, but puppies leave very little room for error.
>
> If you choose a commercial fresh, freeze-dried or raw food, check the label says complete for growth or all life stages. And for a large breed, it should say it's suitable for large-breed puppies.
>
> If you cook at home or make your own raw food, please get a recipe from a veterinary nutritionist. A puppy recipe that's out by a little in calcium can cause real harm in a few months.
>
> Raw feeding also needs extra hygiene, because puppies' immune systems are still developing.
>
> Feed three to four meals a day until about six months, then two to three. Weigh your puppy weekly.
>
> And your vet is your partner all year: vaccines, worming and growth checks.

**Check question:** What should a puppy food label say?

- "Natural"

- "Grain-free"

- **"Complete for growth" or "all life stages" (and large breed if relevant) (correct)**

- "Adult maintenance"

**Feedback:** Exactly. Growth needs its own formula.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** Why shouldn't you add calcium to a complete puppy food?

- Calcium is toxic

- **Too much calcium can disturb bone development, especially in large breeds (correct)**

- Puppies don't need calcium

- It makes food taste bad

*Correct:* Yes. More isn't better for growing bones. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Excess calcium can harm growing bones.

**Q2.** What's the goal for large-breed puppy growth?

- Grow as fast as possible

- **Slow, steady growth and a lean body (correct)**

- Reach adult weight by 4 months

- Stay as small as possible

*Correct:* Right. Slow growth protects joints. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Slow, steady growth is safer.

### Screen 5 · Write — Your puppy feeding plan

**Practice intro:** Write the basics in one place.

**Instructions:** Note breed, expected adult size, food (and label wording), meals per day, and weekly weight.

**Weak example:** "Puppy food, as much as she wants."

**Why it's weak:** Free feeding can mean fast growth and overweight, and "puppy food" may not suit a large breed.

**Placeholder:** Breed:… / Expected adult weight:… / Food and label:… / Meals per day:… / Weight log:…

**Model answer:** "Breed: Labrador. Expected adult weight: 30 kg. Food: commercial fresh food, 'complete for growth, including large-breed puppies'. Meals: 3 a day until 6 months. Weight log: every Saturday; check with vet at each visit. No added calcium."

**Why it works:** Right food for size, structured meals, and monitoring.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** Start weekly growth tracking.

1.  Weigh your puppy (hold them on a bathroom scale and subtract your weight, or use a baby scale).

2.  Check body condition: ribs easy to feel, visible waist (Lesson 15).

3.  Save weight and a photo in the app.

4.  Repeat every week and share the chart with your vet.

No puppy at home? Save this lesson for later, or share it with a friend who has one.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Feeding by expected adult size

- **Small breeds** (under 10 kg) grow fast and finish growing earlier (around 9–10 months). Tiny puppies can get low blood sugar if they skip meals; feed little and often.

- **Medium breeds** finish around 12 months.

- **Large and giant breeds** grow until 15–24 months. They often stay on large-breed puppy food longer.

Ask your vet when to switch to adult food. Switching too early or too late both have downsides.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**See your vet if your puppy:**

- Limps, has swollen joints, or seems in pain

- Grows much faster or slower than expected, or is too thin or chubby

- Has diarrhoea or vomiting for more than a few hours, especially if very young (puppies dehydrate fast)

- Is weak, wobbly, trembling or very sleepy (possible low blood sugar, especially toy breeds: call now)

**For the next 24 hours:** weigh your puppy and check the food label.

**Key takeaways:**

- Puppies need food labelled complete for growth.

- Calcium must be balanced: not too little, not too much.

- Large breeds should grow slowly and stay lean.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Growth Guide

**Progress:** Lesson 20 of 30 complete · Day 20 streak

**Celebration text:** Two thirds done! You know how to build a strong body from day one. Next: caring for your dog in their golden years.

## Lesson 21 — Senior Dogs

About 9 minutes. Vet review: High.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** The grey muzzle years

The muzzle is going grey. Walks are a bit shorter, naps a bit longer. Your dog still meets you at the door, just a few seconds later than before.

The senior years can be some of the sweetest. With the right food, care and attention, many dogs stay bright and comfortable well into old age. Today you'll learn how needs change, and what to watch for.

**In this lesson:**

- When a dog becomes "senior"

- How nutrition needs change

- The common health changes to watch

- A simple senior checklist

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

**When is "senior"?** Roughly the last quarter of expected lifespan: around 7–8 years for large and giant breeds, 9–11 for medium and small breeds, but every dog is different.

**How needs change:**

- **Calories:** many seniors need fewer calories, as they're less active; others, especially very old dogs, struggle to keep weight on. Watch the body condition score.

- **Protein:** healthy seniors usually need **as much or more** high-quality protein to protect muscle. Don't cut protein unless your vet advises it, for example for some kidney conditions.

- **Omega-3s:** support joints, brain and kidneys (Lessons 9, 13, 28).

- **Antioxidants:** vitamins E and C and plant polyphenols may support the ageing brain.

- **Digestibility and water:** fresh, wet and rehydrated foods are easier to eat and add moisture.

**Common changes:** arthritis, dental disease, kidney and heart disease, lumps, reduced hearing and sight, and cognitive changes (Lesson 28).

### Screen 3 · Listen — Little changes, big comfort

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> With senior dogs, small changes make a big difference.
>
> Make food easy to eat. If teeth are sore or appetite is low, soften food, warm it a little, or add broth. Fresh and rehydrated freeze-dried foods are often welcomed.
>
> Split meals. Two or three smaller meals are easier to digest.
>
> Keep water close. Older dogs may not get up as often to drink. Put an extra bowl near their bed.
>
> Keep them moving. Shorter, more frequent walks and gentle sniffing games keep muscles and minds active.
>
> And see your vet twice a year. Many problems that come with age, like kidney disease or dental pain, are easier to manage when caught early. A blood and urine test can show changes before your dog does.

**Check question:** How often should senior dogs usually see the vet?

- Only when sick

- Every 3 years

- **About twice a year (correct)**

- Never, if they seem fine

**Feedback:** Right. Early detection matters most in older dogs.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** Do healthy senior dogs need less protein?

- Yes, always

- **No, they usually need as much or more high-quality protein (correct)**

- They need no protein

- Only plant protein

*Correct:* Yes. Protein protects muscle as dogs age. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Healthy seniors usually need plenty of quality protein.

**Q2.** What's a helpful change for a senior with sore teeth?

- Harder food

- **Softened, warmed or rehydrated food (correct)**

- Fewer meals

- Bigger chews

*Correct:* Right. Easier to eat, more comfortable. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Softer food helps sore mouths, and see your vet.

### Screen 5 · Write — What's changed?

**Practice intro:** Compare your dog now with a year ago.

**Instructions:** Write what's changed in eating, drinking, sleeping, walking, behaviour and body.

**Weak example:** "He's just slower."

**Why it's weak:** "Slower" hides details that could matter.

**Placeholder:** Eating:… / Drinking:… / Sleep:… / Walks:… / Behaviour:… / Body (lumps, weight):…

**Model answer:** "Eating: slower, drops food from the left side. Drinking: more than before. Sleep: restless at night. Walks: 30 min down to 20. Behaviour: barks at nothing in the evening. Body: new soft lump on the chest, weight down 1 kg. Book a senior check and mention all of this."

**Why it works:** Detailed changes give the vet real clues.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**The senior checklist.** Tick what applies and save it in the app:

- Weight and body condition stable?

- Eating normally, no dropping food or chewing on one side?

- Drinking and peeing as usual?

- Getting up and walking comfortably?

- New lumps? (Measure and photograph them.)

- Sleeping well at night?

- Recognising people and finding their way around?

- Breath and teeth OK?

Any "no" is worth mentioning at your next vet visit, or sooner if it's new or getting worse.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Lumps and bumps

Many lumps in older dogs are harmless fatty lumps, but some aren't, and you can't tell by touch. Vets often take a quick needle sample. A simple rule: see your vet about any new lump, any lump that grows, changes, or bleeds, and any lump bigger than a pea that has been there for a month.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**See your vet promptly if your senior dog:**

- Drinks or pees much more, or has accidents

- Loses weight or muscle, or stops eating

- Has a new or growing lump

- Coughs, breathes fast at rest, faints or tires very quickly

- Seems confused, gets lost at home, or changes sleep patterns

**For the next 24 hours:** complete the senior checklist.

**Key takeaways:**

- Seniors usually need as much quality protein, plus omega-3s.

- Make food easy to eat and water easy to reach.

- Twice-yearly vet checks catch problems early.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Golden Years Guardian

**Progress:** Lesson 21 of 30 complete · Day 21 streak

**Celebration text:** Three weeks strong. You're ready to make every senior year a good one. Next: feeding pregnant and nursing dogs.

## Lesson 22 — Pregnant & Nursing Dogs

About 9 minutes. Vet review: Critical.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** Eating for a whole litter

She's quieter than usual, her belly is rounding, and she's started making a nest in the laundry basket. Puppies are on the way.

Pregnancy and nursing are the most demanding times in a dog's life. A nursing mother can need two to four times her normal energy. Get the food right and you support her and every puppy. Get it wrong, and some well-meant ideas can be dangerous.

**In this lesson:**

- How needs change through pregnancy and nursing

- Why extra calcium in pregnancy is risky

- What to feed and how often

- Warning signs that need a vet now

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

Dog pregnancy lasts about **63 days** (9 weeks).

| Stage                           | Needs                                                      | Feeding                                                                                                               |
|---------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Weeks 1–5                       | Close to normal                                            | Usual amount of a complete diet; keep her lean                                                                        |
| Weeks 6–9                       | Rising, up to about 25–50% more by the end                 | Gradually increase; switch to a food for growth or all life stages, as your vet advises; smaller, more frequent meals |
| Nursing (weeks 1–4 after birth) | Peaks at 2–4 times normal energy, depending on litter size | Free access or 3–4+ meals a day; lots of fresh water                                                                  |
| Weaning                         | Falling                                                    | Reduce gradually as puppies eat solid food                                                                            |

**Calcium: the critical rule.** **Do not add calcium supplements during pregnancy** unless your vet specifically prescribes them. Extra calcium during pregnancy can make the body less able to release its own calcium stores when milk production starts, raising the risk of **eclampsia** (dangerously low blood calcium) during nursing. Small breeds and large litters are at higher risk.

### Screen 3 · Listen — Planning together with your vet

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> If your dog is pregnant, your vet should guide the whole journey. Here's what I'd discuss.
>
> The food. Most mothers do well on a high-quality, complete food made for growth or all life stages, from late pregnancy through nursing. It's energy-dense and has the right calcium and phosphorus.
>
> If she eats a home-cooked or raw diet, this is the time to have it checked by a veterinary nutritionist. Pregnancy and nursing leave no room for gaps.
>
> Meals. In late pregnancy, her belly is crowded. Smaller, more frequent meals help.
>
> Weight. Weigh her weekly. Gaining too much can make birth harder; losing weight while nursing is a sign she needs more food.
>
> Water. Nursing mothers need a lot. Keep fresh water always next to her.
>
> And no supplements without your vet, especially calcium and vitamin D.

**Check question:** Should you add calcium during pregnancy?

- Yes, as much as possible

- **No, not unless your vet prescribes it (correct)**

- Only in the first week

- Only bone meal

**Feedback:** Exactly. Extra calcium in pregnancy can raise the risk of eclampsia.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** When do a mother dog's energy needs peak?

- First month of pregnancy

- **During nursing, especially weeks 3–4 after birth (correct)**

- Before mating

- After weaning

*Correct:* Yes. Milk production demands the most energy. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Nursing is the peak.

**Q2.** What is eclampsia?

- A skin condition

- **Dangerously low blood calcium, usually during nursing (correct)**

- An eye infection

- Overeating

*Correct:* Right. It's an emergency. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Eclampsia is dangerously low blood calcium.

### Screen 5 · Write — Questions for your vet

**Practice intro:** Go to your vet prepared.

**Instructions:** Write the questions you want to ask about feeding through pregnancy and nursing.

**Weak example:** "What should she eat?"

**Why it's weak:** Too general to get specific, useful advice.

**Placeholder:** My questions:…

**Model answer:** "1) Is her current food suitable for late pregnancy and nursing, or should we switch, and when? 2) How much should she gain? 3) How many meals in late pregnancy? 4) Her home-cooked recipe: can it be checked by a vet nutritionist? 5) Any supplements, or none? 6) What signs of eclampsia should I watch for and who do I call at night?"

**Why it works:** Specific, covers the risks, and plans for emergencies.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

If your dog is pregnant: weigh her today, note the week of pregnancy, and save your questions for the vet. If not: save this lesson for the future, or share it with a breeder or friend who needs it.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Feeding the puppies through weaning

Puppies start showing interest in food at about 3–4 weeks. Offer a soft gruel of a complete puppy food (soaked, mashed, or a commercial fresh puppy food) several times a day. Mother's milk gradually decreases as puppies eat more, usually with weaning complete around 6–8 weeks. Reduce the mother's food gradually as her milk decreases, to help her milk dry up comfortably.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Emergency (call your vet now) if a pregnant or nursing dog shows:**

- Restlessness, panting, trembling, muscle twitching, stiffness, wobbliness, high temperature or seizures (possible eclampsia)

- Strong straining for 30 minutes with no puppy, more than 2 hours between puppies, or green-black discharge before the first puppy

- Hot, hard, red or painful teats (possible mastitis)

- Refusing food for more than a day, vomiting, or lethargy

**For the next 24 hours:** if relevant, weigh her and book a pregnancy check.

**Key takeaways:**

- Needs rise in late pregnancy and peak during nursing.

- No calcium supplements in pregnancy unless prescribed.

- Know the signs of eclampsia and act fast.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Mother's Helper

**Progress:** Lesson 22 of 30 complete · Day 22 streak

**Celebration text:** You know how to support a mother and her litter safely. Next: feeding for recovery after surgery.

## Lesson 23 — Recovery After Surgery

About 8 minutes. Vet review: Critical.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** Home from the vet, wearing a cone

You bring your dog home after surgery. They're sleepy, a little wobbly, wearing a cone, and looking at you as if you've betrayed them. You want to do everything right.

Rest, the vet's instructions, and the right food all help the body heal. Today you'll learn how to feed and care for a recovering dog, and the warning signs that mean a call to the vet.

**In this lesson:**

- What healing needs from food

- The first 24–48 hours

- Getting appetite back

- Warning signs

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

Healing a wound takes energy and building blocks:

- **Protein** for new tissue and the immune system. Getting enough protein is one of the most important parts of recovery.

- **Energy (calories)** so the body doesn't break down muscle.

- **Zinc, vitamin A and vitamin C** for skin and wound repair (a complete diet usually covers these).

- **Omega-3s** (EPA and DHA) are studied for supporting a healthy response to inflammation.

- **Water** for every process in the body.

**Always follow your vet's discharge instructions first.** After some surgeries (for example on the stomach or intestines, or for pancreatitis), your vet will prescribe a specific diet and feeding schedule.

### Screen 3 · Listen — The first two days

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> The first night home, anaesthetic can make dogs sleepy and a little queasy.
>
> Offer a small meal: about a quarter to half the usual amount, unless your vet says otherwise. Bland and easy to digest is best. Many vets suggest the dog's usual food in a small amount, or a vet recovery diet.
>
> Don't worry if they don't eat much the first evening. But they should start eating within about 24 hours.
>
> Keep water close, especially with a cone. Some dogs can't reach the bowl. A shallow, wide bowl, raised a little, helps.
>
> Don't start a new food or supplement now. This isn't the time for changes.
>
> From day two, if all is well, build back up to normal portions over a couple of days. And keep them calm: short lead walks only, no jumping or running, until your vet says so.

**Check question:** When should a dog start eating after surgery?

- Within an hour

- **Usually within about 24 hours; call the vet if not (correct)**

- After a week

- Only when the stitches come out

**Feedback:** Right. Appetite is an important sign.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** Which nutrient is especially important for wound healing?

- Sugar

- **Protein (correct)**

- Salt

- Caffeine

*Correct:* Yes. Protein builds new tissue. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Protein is key for healing.

**Q2.** Is recovery a good time to switch to a new food?

- Yes, a fresh start

- **No, avoid changes unless your vet prescribes a diet (correct)**

- Only to raw food

- Only to puppy food

*Correct:* Right. Keep things stable. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Avoid changes during recovery.

### Screen 5 · Write — Recovery log

**Practice intro:** A simple log helps you and your vet.

**Instructions:** Write the details for one day of recovery.

**Weak example:** "Seems OK."

**Why it's weak:** It misses the details that show healing or a problem.

**Placeholder:** Food eaten:… / Water:… / Pee and poop:… / Wound:… / Energy and pain:… / Medicines given:…

**Model answer:** "Food: half breakfast, all dinner. Water: drinking normally. Pee: yes, 3 times; poop: not yet (day 1). Wound: clean, dry, slight pinkness, no swelling. Energy: sleepy, wagged when I came home; no crying. Medicines: pain relief at 8:00 and 20:00 as prescribed."

**Why it works:** It covers everything your vet would ask on the phone.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

No surgery planned? Prepare a recovery kit: a shallow water bowl, a comfortable quiet bed space, a lick mat (for gentle, calm enrichment), and your vet's out-of-hours number saved in your phone and in the app. If your dog is recovering now: check the wound twice a day and fill in the log.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Calm recovery

Rest is part of healing, but bored dogs get restless. Gentle brain games help: lick mats with part of the meal, sniffing a few hidden pieces of food in a towel, short calm training ("touch", "look"). Avoid chews that need hard work if the surgery was on the mouth. Keep the cone on as your vet advises; licking can open wounds and cause infection.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**Call your vet if your dog:**

- Hasn't eaten within 24 hours of coming home

- Vomits more than once, or has diarrhoea

- Has a wound that is very red, swollen, hot, smells, oozes, bleeds or opens

- Is very lethargic, in pain despite medication, or breathing fast

- Hasn't peed within 24 hours, or pooed within 2–3 days

**Emergency:** pale gums, collapse, a swollen hard belly, or heavy bleeding. Go now.

**For the next 24 hours:** make your recovery kit or fill in the log.

**Key takeaways:**

- Follow your vet's discharge instructions first.

- Small, easy meals first; protein and water support healing.

- Watch appetite, the wound and energy closely.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Recovery Ready

**Progress:** Lesson 23 of 30 complete · Day 23 streak

**Celebration text:** You're prepared for the days when your dog needs you most. Next: an honest look at diet and cancer.

## Lesson 24 — Diet and Cancer: What We Know

About 10 minutes. Vet review: Critical.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** The word nobody wants to hear

It might be a lump found during a cuddle, or a test result on a vet's screen. Cancer is common in older dogs, and when it comes, owners often turn to one question: *what should I feed?*

The internet is full of loud answers, from "sugar feeds cancer" to "this diet cures it". The truth is quieter and more useful. Today you'll learn what the evidence really says, so you can make calm, informed choices together with your vet.

**In this lesson:**

- What the Warburg effect is, and what it doesn't prove

- The research on diet and cancer in dogs

- What good nutrition can do during cancer

- Questions to ask your vet

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

**The Warburg effect:** in the 1920s, Otto Warburg noticed that many cancer cells use glucose (sugar) for energy in an unusual way, even when oxygen is available. This led to the idea that cutting carbohydrates could "starve" cancer.

**What the evidence shows:**

- Cancer cells can use other fuels too, including fat and some amino acids.

- The body keeps blood sugar steady even on a very low-carbohydrate diet, by making its own glucose.

- In dogs, one often-cited study (Ogilvie and colleagues, 2000) found that dogs with lymphoma fed a diet higher in fat and enriched with omega-3s, and lower in carbohydrate, during chemotherapy had longer disease-free periods in some groups. It was one study with a specific cancer and treatment.

- There is **no good evidence that any diet cures cancer in dogs**, or that a diet alone shrinks tumours.

**What we do know:** many dogs with cancer lose weight and muscle (cancer cachexia), and this worsens outcomes. Keeping a dog well fed, at a healthy weight, with enough protein, is one of the most helpful things you can do.

### Screen 3 · Listen — Hope and honesty

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> I want to be honest with you, because you deserve that.
>
> No food can cure cancer. Anyone who tells you otherwise is selling something.
>
> But food matters a lot. A dog who keeps eating, keeps their weight and muscle, and enjoys their meals, often copes better with treatment and has a better quality of life.
>
> Many veterinary oncologists favour diets that are tasty, energy-dense, with high-quality protein, moderate to higher fat and fewer simple carbohydrates, often with omega-3s. Fresh, home-cooked or raw diets can sometimes fit this, if they're balanced and safe for your dog.
>
> One important caution: dogs having chemotherapy can have weaker immune systems. Many oncologists advise against raw food during treatment because of the bacteria risk. Please ask yours.
>
> Your vet and you are a team. Bring your questions. Bring your ideas. Decide together.

**Check question:** What's one of the most helpful things food can do for a dog with cancer?

- Cure the cancer

- Starve the tumour completely

- **Help maintain weight, muscle and quality of life (correct)**

- Replace treatment

**Feedback:** Exactly. Keeping strength matters.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** Does cutting carbohydrates "starve" cancer?

- Yes, completely

- **No, cancer cells can use other fuels, and the body makes its own glucose (correct)**

- Only on raw diets

- Only in small dogs

*Correct:* Right. The idea is too simple. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Cancer cells adapt, and the body keeps blood sugar steady.

**Q2.** Why might raw food be paused during chemotherapy?

- Raw food stops chemotherapy working

- **Weakened immunity increases the risk from bacteria in raw meat (correct)**

- Dogs dislike raw food during treatment

- Raw food has no protein

*Correct:* Yes. Ask your oncologist. *Incorrect:* Not quite. It's about infection risk when immunity is low.

### Screen 5 · Write — Questions for your vet

**Practice intro:** Go to the appointment ready.

**Instructions:** Write the questions you'd ask about feeding a dog with cancer.

**Weak example:** "Which diet cures cancer?"

**Why it's weak:** It's based on a myth and won't get a useful answer.

**Placeholder:** My questions:…

**Model answer:** "1) What's my dog's ideal weight now, and how do we keep muscle? 2) Is his current diet suitable during treatment? 3) Should we pause raw food during chemotherapy? 4) Would omega-3s help, and at what dose? 5) What do we do if he stops eating? 6) Are any supplements risky with his treatment?"

**Why it works:** Practical, safe, and treatment-aware.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

Write your own list of questions in the app, whether or not your dog has cancer. Add your dog's current weight and body condition score. If your dog has cancer, also note their appetite each day this week. Appetite and weight are among the first things your vet will want to know.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Supplements and treatment

Some supplements may interact with cancer treatment. For example, high doses of certain antioxidants are debated during some chemotherapy and radiation treatments, because these treatments partly work by causing oxidative damage to cancer cells. Some herbs can affect how the liver handles drugs. This doesn't mean all supplements are harmful, only that **every supplement should be discussed with your vet or oncologist** before starting.

### Screen 8 · Vet check + today's practice

**See your vet if you notice:**

- A new lump, or one that grows or changes

- Weight loss without trying, or loss of appetite

- Sores that don't heal, bleeding or discharge from any opening

- Difficulty eating, swallowing, breathing, peeing or pooing

- Ongoing lameness, low energy, or a persistent cough

**For the next 24 hours:** write your questions and record weight and BCS.

**Key takeaways:**

- No diet cures cancer; be wary of anyone who claims it.

- Good nutrition helps keep weight, muscle and quality of life.

- Discuss every diet change and supplement with your vet.

### Screen 9 · Achievement

**Badge:** Clear Thinker

**Progress:** Lesson 24 of 30 complete · Day 24 streak

**Celebration text:** You've chosen honesty over hype, a gift to your dog. Next: everyday steps that support a healthy home.

## Lesson 25 — Cancer-Aware Feeding & Toxin Audit

About 9 minutes. Vet review: Critical.

### Screen 1 · Read — The hook

**Headline:** A healthier home, one shelf at a time

Your dog's nose is about a foot from the floor. They lick their paws after walking on the lawn, drink from puddles, and lie on freshly cleaned carpets. Their world is lower, closer and more direct than ours.

No home can be risk-free, and most cancers have many causes, including genes and age. But there are sensible, low-cost steps that reduce exposure to some known risks. Today you'll learn them, plus foods with interesting early research.

**In this lesson:**

- Known environmental risks for dogs

- Foods being researched: omega-3s, broccoli sprouts, turkey tail

- A practical home toxin audit

- Keeping perspective

### Screen 2 · Read — The science, simply

**Exposures linked to cancer risk in dogs in research studies:**

- **Second-hand tobacco smoke:** linked to nasal and lung cancers in some studies.

- **Some lawn and garden chemicals:** some studies, especially in Scottish Terriers, have linked lawn herbicide exposure to bladder cancer.

- **Excess sun on light, thin-coated skin:** certain skin cancers.

- **Obesity:** linked to many health problems; a healthy weight is protective in many ways.

**Foods under research:**

- **Omega-3s (EPA and DHA):** studied for supporting a healthy inflammatory response and muscle maintenance (Lessons 9 and 24).

- **Broccoli sprouts:** rich in sulforaphane, which is studied in the lab for its effects on cell defence pathways. Evidence in dogs is very limited. A small amount of lightly steamed or fresh sprouts is safe for most dogs.

- **Turkey tail mushroom:** a small pilot study in dogs with haemangiosarcoma, given alongside surgery, reported encouraging survival times. It was small and early, and not a treatment on its own.

- **Vegetables in general:** one study in Scottish Terriers found that those eating green-leafy or yellow-orange vegetables at least three times a week had a lower risk of bladder cancer.

**Remember:** these are about lowering some risks and supporting health, never about treating or curing cancer.

### Screen 3 · Listen — Small changes, done calmly

**Audio script (Agota, ~80 sec):**

> I don't want this lesson to make you afraid. I want it to make you feel capable.
>
> Start with the big, easy wins. If anyone smokes, please do it outside, away from your dog.
>
> Look at the garden. Could you skip weedkillers and chemical lawn treatments where your dog walks, or keep your dog off treated grass until it's dry, as the label says?
>
> Wipe paws after walks. A damp cloth removes whatever was on the pavement or park grass.
>
> Store cleaning products and chemicals out of reach, and let floors dry before your dog walks on them.
>
> Add a few vegetables, like steamed greens or a sprinkle of broccoli sprouts, to a complete diet.
>
> And keep your dog lean.
>
> These steps are small, cheap, and good for the whole family, not just your dog.

**Check question:** What's one easy way to reduce chemical exposure after walks?

- Bathe the dog daily

- **Wipe paws with a damp cloth (correct)**

- Avoid all walks

- Use perfume on the paws

**Feedback:** Exactly. Simple and effective.

### Screen 4 · Quick check

**Q1.** Which exposure has been linked to nasal and lung cancers in some dog studies?

- Rain

- **Second-hand tobacco smoke (correct)**

- Tennis balls

- Cold water

*Correct:* Yes. Smoke outdoors, away from pets. *Incorrect:* Not quite. Second-hand smoke is the one linked in studies.

**Q2.** What does the research on broccoli sprouts in dogs show?

- They cure cancer

- **Interesting lab research, but very limited evidence in dogs (correct)**

- They're toxic

- Nothing has been studied

*Correct:* Right. Promising, not proven. *Incorrect:* Not quite. It's early research, not proof.

### Screen 5 · Write — Your toxin audit

**Practice intro:** Walk through your home and garden with fresh eyes.

**Instructions:** Note one thing in each area you could change.

**Weak example:** "Everything's fine."

**Why it's weak:** It skips the check; small risks are easy to miss.

**Placeholder:** Air:… / Floors and cleaning:… / Garden:… / Walks:… / Food and water bowls:… / Sun:…

**Model answer:** "Air: guests smoke on the balcony; ask them to go further from the door. Floors: keep dog out until floor cleaner dries. Garden: stop using weedkiller on the lawn. Walks: wipe paws when we get home. Bowls: replace scratched plastic bowl with stainless steel. Sun: shade for my white-coated dog on the patio."

**Why it works:** Concrete, small, doable changes in every area.

### Screen 6 · Do it with your dog

**Task:** Do the home toxin audit.

1.  Walk room by room, then the garden.

2.  Note one change per area (use the placeholder).

3.  Pick the two easiest changes and do them this week.

4.  Save your audit in the app.

### Screen 7 · Go deeper — Keeping perspective
